Sample Path Large Deviations for Multivariate Heavy-Tailed Hawkes Processes and Related Lévy Processes
Jose Blanchet, Roger J. A. Laeven, Xingyu Wang, Bert Zwart

Abstract
In this paper, we develop sample path large deviations for multivariate Hawkes processes with heavy-tailed mutual excitation rates. Our results address a broad class of rare events in Hawkes processes at the sample path level and, via the cluster representation of Hawkes processes and a recent result on the tail asymptotics of the cluster sizes, unravel the most likely configurations of (multiple) large clusters that could trigger the target events. Our proof hinges on establishing the asymptotic equivalence, in terms of M-convergence, between a suitably scaled multivariate Hawkes process and a coupled Lévy process with multivariate hidden regular variation. Hence, along the way, we derive a sample path large deviations principle for a class of Lévy processes with multivariate hidden regular variation, which not only plays an auxiliary role in our analysis but is also of independent interest.



1
Introduction

The growing interconnectedness and complexity of modern ecological, economic, engineered, and social systems
have led to risks and uncertainties that can amplify and cascade across populations, markets, and networks
through temporal and spatial feedback.
As a result, understanding and managing the amplification and cascade of risks poses significant challenges across science, engineering, and business.
Multivariate Hawkes processes
offer a formalized approach to model such dependencies and mutual excitation effects in risks,
and
have been widely applied in
finance (
[
3
,
4
,
41
]
),
neuroscience and biology (
[
74
,
52
,
62
]
),
seismology (
[
55
,
45
]
),
epidemiology (
[
22
]
),
social science (
[
56
,
24
,
57
,
64
]
),
queueing systems (
[
21
,
28
,
67
]
),
and cyber security (
[
7
,
10
]
).

Specifically,
this paper focuses on

\bm{N}(t)=(N_{1}(t),N_{2}(t),\ldots,N_{d}(t))^{\top}
,
a
d
-dimensional càdlàg point process
with initial value
\bm{N}(0)=\bm{0}
,
and its conditional intensities
h^{\bm{N}}_{i}
along each dimension are specified as follows
(here, we write
[d]=\{1,2,\ldots,d\}
).

Definition 1.1

(Multivariate Hawkes Processes)
.

The point process
\bm{N}(t)=(N_{i}(t))_{i\in[d]}
satisfies (for each
i\in[d]
and
t\geq 0
)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(N_{i}(t+\Delta)-N_{i}(t)=0\ \big|\ \mathcal{F}_{t}\big)

\displaystyle=1-h^{\bm{N}}_{i}(t)\Delta+\mathit{o}(\Delta),

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(N_{i}(t+\Delta)-N_{i}(t)=1\ \big|\ \mathcal{F}_{t}\big)

\displaystyle=h^{\bm{N}}_{i}(t)\Delta+\mathit{o}(\Delta),

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(N_{i}(t+\Delta)-N_{i}(t)>1\ \big|\ \mathcal{F}_{t}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathit{o}(\Delta),

as
\Delta\downarrow 0
,
with

\mathcal{F}_{t}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sigma\big\{\bm{N}(s):\ s\in[0,t]\big\}
,
and the conditional intensities take the form

\displaystyle h^{\bm{N}}_{i}(t)=c^{\bm{N}}_{i}+\sum_{j\in[d]}\int_{0}^{t}{\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}(s)}f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(t-s)dN_{j}(s),\qquad\forall i\in[d].

(1.1)

Here,
the
c^{\bm{N}}_{i}
’s are positive constants.
For each pair
(i,j)\in[d]^{2}
,

f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(\cdot)
is a deterministic, non-negative, and integrable function,
and
\big(\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}(s)\big)_{s>0}
are independent copies of some non-negative random variables
\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}
(mutually independent across
i,j\in[d]
).

Intuitively speaking, the constant
c^{\bm{N}}_{i}
represents the base rate at which type-
i
immigrant events arrive,
and the random function
\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(\cdot)

dictates the rate at which any type-
j
event induces (i.e., gives birth to) type-
i
events in the future, thus capturing the mutual excitation mechanism of risks.
In Definition
1.1
, the
\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}
’s are typically referred to as excitation rates, and the
f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(\cdot)
’s are called decay functions.
See also
[
25
,
42
]
, and Chapter 7 of
[
26
]
for a detailed treatment of the conditional intensity approach to Hawkes processes.

In this paper, we establish a sample path large deviations principle (LDP) for the multivariate Hawkes process
\bm{N}(t)
, in the presence of power-law heavy tails in the distribution of the mutual excitation rates
\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}
.
In particular, our work resolves significant gaps in the existing literature on large deviations of heavy-tailed Hawkes processes.

•

Existing results are manifestations of
the principle of a single big jump

(e.g.,
[
49
,
6
,
2
]
),
addressing only a limited class of rare events that are driven by
a single point inducing a disproportionately large number of offspring in one generation.
By contrast, our results capture a much broader class of events caused by multiple big jumps across different components.

•

A
sample-path level
characterization of large deviations is absent, even in the univariate setting;
see, for instance,
[
6
]
.
To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to establish sample path LDP for multivariate heavy-tailed Hawkes processes.
It is also worth noting that Hawkes processes can be viewed as continuous-time analogs of branching processes with immigration.
For heavy-tailed branching processes with immigration,
large deviations in the stationary distribution and partial sums have been studied (e.g.,
[
8
,
38
]
);
however, sample path large deviations have still not been characterized, even in the univariate case.

To further explain these gaps and our methodology,
we briefly review the
cluster approach
(also known as the Galton-Watson approach),
which is standard in large deviations analysis of Hawkes processes and exploits the underlying branching structure in
\bm{N}(t)
.
More precisely, a type-
j
immigrant gives birth to children along different dimensions and hence induces its own family tree (i.e., cluster).
The cluster size vectors
\bm{S}_{j}
solve the distributional fixed-point equations

\displaystyle\bm{S}_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.01042pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$}}\hskip-3.01042pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bm{e}_{j}+\sum_{i\in[d]}\sum_{m=1}^{B_{i\leftarrow j}}\bm{S}_{i}^{(m)},\qquad j\in[d],

(1.2)

where

\bm{e}_{j}
is the
j^{\text{th}}
unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e., the vector with its
j^{\text{th}}
entry equal to 1 and all other entries equal to 0),
each
\bm{S}_{i}^{(m)}
is an independent copy of
\bm{S}_{i}
,
and
B_{i\leftarrow j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.01042pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$}}\hskip-3.01042pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\text{Poisson}\big(\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}\left\lVert f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}\right\rVert_{1}\big)

with
\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}
and
f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}
being the excitation rates and decay functions in Definition
1.1

and
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\int|f(x)|dx
.
For more details about the cluster representation of Hawkes processes,
we refer the reader to
[
42
,
54
,
26
]

(see also Definition
E.4
in the Appendix).
Here, we note that the canonical representation of
\bm{S}_{j}
in (
1.2
) is the total progeny of
a multi-type branching process (see, e.g.,
[
47
,
39
]
) across the
d
dimensions, where
B_{i\leftarrow j}
is the count of type-
i
children in one generation from a type-
j
parent.

Specializing to the large deviations analysis for Hawkes processes,
the cluster approach proceeds by first characterizing the behavior of clusters, and then connecting the large deviations of the Hawkes process to those of a compound Poisson
process the increments of which admit the law of the cluster size vectors
\bm{S}_{j}
.
In the light-tailed case,
this approach is streamlined by the classical LDP framework (
[
29
,
31
,
35
,
70
]
)
and has been broadly successful in large deviations analyses of Hawkes processes and several extensions, including marked Hawkes processes and compound Hawkes processes;
see, e.g.,
[
13
,
68
,
48
,
50
]
.
We also refer the reader to
[
76
,
75
]

for large deviations of the non-linear generalization of Hawkes processes (
[
16
]
), where the cluster representation fails.

By contrast,
large deviations analyses of heavy-tailed Hawkes processes are relatively scarce,
with the two aforementioned gaps persisting.
To resolve the associated technical challenges,
we implement the cluster approach in the multivariate heavy-tailed setting through three steps.
In the first step, we apply the recent progress in
[
12
]
to characterize the tail behavior of the cluster size vector
\bm{S}_{i}
.
To be more specific,
given some non-empty index set
\bm{j}\subseteq[d]
, let

\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{\sum_{i\in\bm{j}}w_{i}\mathbf{E}[\bm{S}_{i}]:\ w_{i}\geq 0\ \forall i\in\bm{j}\}
be the convex cone generated by the vectors
(\mathbf{E}[\bm{S}_{i}])_{i\in\bm{j}}
.
We show that, over each cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
,
Theorem 3.2 of
[
12
]
entails that
the measure
\mathbf{P}\big(n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in\ \cdot\ \cap\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})\big)
—characterizing the tail behavior of
\bm{S}_{i}
when restricted over the cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
—is roughly decaying at a power-law rate
n^{-\alpha(\bm{j})}
as
n\to\infty
.
That is,
hidden regular variation
(e.g.,
[
59
,
53
]
) arises endogenously in the cluster size vector
\bm{S}_{i}
, which exhibits varying degrees of heavy-tailedness across different directions in Euclidean space.
We review in Section
2.2
the precise definitions of the tail indices
\alpha(\bm{j})
and the rigorous statements for tail asymptotics of
\bm{S}_{i}
.
Compared to prior results (e.g.,
[
2
,
49
]
),
such characterizations are able to address a much more general class of sets
A
for the asymptotic analysis of rare events
\{n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in A\}
,
revealing that

the extremal behaviors of Hawkes process clusters are driven by multiple big jumps

(i.e., multiple points in
\bm{N}(t)
giving birth to a disproportionately large number of offspring),
whose contributions
align with vectors
\big(\mathbf{E}[\bm{S}_{l}]\big)_{l\in[d]}
.

In light of the cluster size tail asymptotics,
our second step is to develop a general framework for
sample path large deviations
under increments exhibiting
multivariate hidden regular variation
.
Sample path level characterizations for the principle of a single big jump (e.g.,
[
44
,
15
,
30
,
33
,
37
]
)
and the more general multiple big jump principle
(e.g.,
[
14
,
63
,
9
]
)
are available for random walks and Lévy processes with regularly varying increments.
See also
[
32
]
for the characterization of the multiple big jump principle in point processes,
which could imply sample path LDPs for heavy-tailed random walks or Lévy processes through continuous mapping arguments.
However,
studies on sample path large deviations under increments with
multivariate hidden regular variation
are lacking.
For instance, Result 2 in
[
19
]
addresses Lévy processes with independent jumps of different heavy-tailedness along the standard orthogonal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
However, this is not suitable for our study of Hawkes processes, where the cluster size vectors exhibit strong cross-coordinate correlations and can align with arbitrary directions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Meanwhile, Section 5 of
[
27
]
explores the tail asymptotics of Lévy processes with multivariate hidden regular variation but does not provide sample path level results.

To overcome this technical challenge, we establish in Theorem
3.2
the sample path LDP for Lévy processes under increments that exhibit a suitable, general form of multivariate hidden regular variation.
The results are stated w.r.t. the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology of the càdlàg space
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
.
This development significantly extends existing results:
compared to
[
63
,
9
,
32
]
,
our results address multivariate cases with hidden regular variation;
unlike Theorem 3.3 of
[
63
]
or Result 2 in
[
19
]
, which assume independent increments along an orthogonal basis, we allow for more general dependence structures of multivariate hidden regular variation in Lévy processes;
and in contrast to
[
27
]
, we provide results at the sample path level.
We present formal statements in Section
3.1
and emphasize the following aspects:
(i) the proof refines the notion of asymptotic equivalence in
\mathbb{M}
-convergence theory (
[
53
]
), distinguishing large jumps by their directions to capture hidden regular variation in the increments of Lévy processes;
(ii) the multivariate hidden regular variation is modeled via the
\mathcal{MHRV}
formalism introduced in
[
12
]
, which is particularly well-suited to branching processes and Hawkes processes;
(iii) beyond our application to Hawkes processes, Theorem
3.2
is also of independent interest due to the relevance of Lévy models and hidden regular variation in risk management and mathematical finance (e.g.,
[
17
,
18
,
65
,
27
]
).

The third step concerns applying the framework in Theorem
3.2
to Hawkes processes.
Specifically, we show that for large deviations analysis,
\bm{N}(t)
is asymptotically equivalent to a Lévy process with cluster size vectors
\bm{S}_{j}
as increments (see (
1.2
)).
That is, in terms of extremal behaviors, the sample path of the heavy-tailed Hawkes process remains essentially unchanged even if all arrivals in the same cluster are merged into a single jump
(i.e., if all offspring arrive simultaneously with their immigrant ancestor).
To achieve this, we
(i)
refine the approach in
[
61
]

to obtain tight bounds on the cluster lifetime (i.e., the time gap between an immigrant and its last offspring),
and
(ii)
apply again the notion of asymptotic equivalence in terms of
\mathbb{M}
-convergence.
We outline our proof strategy in Section
3.3
and collect technical tools for
\mathbb{M}
-convergence theory in Section
A
.

Equipped with the tools above,
we
characterize
sample path large deviations for multivariate heavy-tailed Hawkes processes.
More precisely,
under the presence of regular variation in the mutual excitation rates of
\bm{N}(t)
and under proper tail conditions for the decay functions
f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(\cdot)
,
Theorem
3.3
establishes asymptotics of the form

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{k}(A)}(A^{\circ})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in A\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}(A)}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in A\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}(A)}(n)}\leq{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{k}(A)}(A^{-})

(1.3)

for general sets
A\subseteq\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
.
Here,

A^{\circ}
and
A^{-}
are the interior and closure of
A
w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of the càdlàg space
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
;

\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{\bm{N}(nt)/n:\ t\geq 0\}

is the scaled sample path of
\bm{N}(t)
embedded in
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
,
the limiting measures
\big({\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{k}}(\cdot)\big)_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}}
are supported on
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
,
and the rates of decay
\big(\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)\big)_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}}
are regularly varying functions with indices determined by the
\alpha(\bm{j})
’s—the power-law indices for tail asymptotics of Hawkes cluster sizes.
In particular, the rate function
\bm{k}(A)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}
solves a discrete optimization problem identifying the
most likely configuration of large clusters
that can drive the Hawkes process
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
into the rare-event set
A
.
Intuitively speaking,
the nominal behavior of the (scaled) Hawkes process
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
is a linear path with slope
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}
(its expected increments under stationarity),
and recall that, by the tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters, the probability of observing a large cluster with size vector lying in the cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
is roughly of order
n^{-\alpha(\bm{j})}
.
The optimization problem behind
\bm{k}(A)
(see Remark
5
) then identifies, among all combinations of large clusters that can push the
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}
-linear path into the set
A
,
the configuration that is most likely to occur.
We provide the rigorous statement of Theorem
3.3
and the precise definitions of the notions involved in Section
3.2
.
Here, we highlight:
(i)
the limiting measures
{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{k}}(\cdot)

can be efficiently computed via Monte Carlo simulation (see Remark
6
);
(ii)
in the univariate case, Theorem
3.3
can be uplifted to the
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,T]

(see Section
E.4
of the Appendix);
(iii)
in the multivariate case,
it is generally not possible to strengthen
the characterizations in (
1.3
) w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
to those on
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
or other stronger Skorokhod non-uniform topologies (see Remark
7
).

Regarding the contributions of this work, we emphasize:
(i) The study of large deviations for heavy-tailed Hawkes processes aligns with the importance of power-law heavy tails in fields like epidemiology (
[
23
]
), queueing systems (
[
2
,
34
]
), and mathematical finance (
[
5
,
40
,
46
,
43
]
);
(ii) This paper addresses major gaps in prior work by developing sample path LDPs for heavy-tailed Hawkes processes that go well beyond the single big jump regime.
For instance, in the context of (unmarked) Hawkes processes,
Proposition 3 of
[
49
]
and Proposition 8.2 of
[
6
]

(see also
[
38
]
for closely related results regarding branching processes with immigration)
are comparable to a special case of (
1.3
) with
A=\{\xi\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty):\ \bm{c}^{\top}\xi(t)>1\}
with some
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
and
t>0
.
That is, they address only a limited class of rare events, which are characterized by an extreme value of the process at a specific time point rather than by the extremal behavior of the entire sample path, and are driven by a single big jump in the Hawkes process.
(We also note that Proposition 8.1 of
[
6
]
studies large deviations of the maximum functional regarding the clusters of marked Hawkes processes, and is another manifestation of the principle of a single big jump.)
In contrast, our Theorem
3.3
covers a much broader class of sample-path-level rare events and reveals the most likely configuration of (multiple) big clusters governing such rare events.
In many applications across finance, machine learning, and operations research, impactful rare events are determined by the entire sample path of the stochastic dynamics rather than just the running maximum or endpoint value,
and are driven by multiple large jumps in the system (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
69
,
36
,
71
]
).
Our results thus lay the foundation of
precise theoretical insights and efficient rare event simulation for practical systems under clustering and mutually exciting risks.

This paper is structured as follows.
Section
2
reviews the tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters. Section
3
presents the main results of this paper.
In the Appendix,
Section
A

collects useful technical tools for the
\mathbb{M}
-convergence theory,
Section
B
collects additional details for tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters,
Section
C
provides the details of the counterexamples in Remarks
7
and
8
,
Sections
D
and
E
contain the proofs for Section
3
,
Section
F
provides sample path large deviations for Lévy Processes with
\mathcal{MHRV}
increments under relaxed conditions, and
Section
G
provides theorem trees to aid readability of the proofs.

2
Preliminaries

This section reviews key definitions and results for our subsequent analysis and is structured as follows.
Section
2.1
reviews
\mathcal{MHRV}
, a notion of multivariate hidden regular variation introduced in
[
12
]
.
Section
2.2
adapts Theorem 3.2 of
[
12
]

to our setting and characterizes the hidden regular variation in Hawkes process clusters in terms of
\mathcal{MHRV}
.
These results are pivotal for the subsequent large-deviation analysis of multivariate Hawkes processes in this paper.

We begin by setting frequently used notations.
Let
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}=[0,\infty)^{d}
,

\mathbb{Z}
be the set of integers,

\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\}

be the set of non-negative integers,
and

\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\cdots\}
be the set of positive integers.
Given a set
X
and a countable set
A
, we adopt notations
\bm{x}\in X^{A}

for vectors of the form
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in A}
that are of length
|A|
, with each coordinate
x_{i}\in X
indexed by elements of
A
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
let
[n]\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
,
with the convention that
[0]=\emptyset
.
For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
{\mathcal{P}}_{m}
be the collection of all
non-empty
subsets of
[m]
, i.e., the power set of
\{1,2,\ldots,m\}
excluding
\emptyset
.
Given some metric space
(\mathbb{S},\bm{d})
, a set
E\subseteq\mathbb{S}
, and
r>0
,
let the closed set

E^{r}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{y\in\mathbb{S}:\ \bm{d}(E,y)\leq r\}
be the
r
-enlargement of the set
E
,
and the open set

E_{r}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}((E^{c})^{r})^{c}=\{y\in\mathbb{S}:\ \bm{d}(E^{c},y)>r\}

be the
r
-shrinkage
of
E
;
besides,
let

E^{\circ}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bigcup_{r>0}E_{r}
and
E^{-}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bigcap_{r>0}E^{r}
be the interior and closure of
E
, respectively.
Throughout this paper,
we consider the
L_{1}
norm
\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert=\sum_{i\in[d]}|x_{i}|
over Euclidean spaces.
For any random element
X
and Borel measurable set
A
,
we use
\mathscr{L}(X)
to denote the law of
X
, and
\mathscr{L}(X|A)
for the conditional law of
X
given the event
A
.
Given real numbers
x
and
y
,
let
x\wedge y\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\min\{x,y\}
,
x\vee y=\max\{x,y\}
be the minimum and maximum operators, respectively,
and let

\lfloor x\rfloor\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\max\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:\ n\leq x\}
,

\lceil x\rceil\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\min\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:\ n\geq x\}

be the floor and ceiling operators, respectively.
Given sequences of non-negative real numbers
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
we say that
x_{n}=\mathcal{O}(y_{n})
(as
n\to\infty
) if there exists some
C\in[0,\infty)
such that
x_{n}\leq Cy_{n}\ \forall n\geq 1
, and that
x_{n}=\mathit{o}(y_{n})
if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}x_{n}/y_{n}=0
.

2.1
Multivariate Hidden Regular Variation

Recall that a measurable function
\phi:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is regularly varying as
x\rightarrow\infty
with index
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, denoted as
\phi(x)\in\mathcal{RV}_{\beta}(x)
as
x\to\infty
, if
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\phi(tx)/\phi(x)=t^{\beta}
holds for any
t>0
.
See, e.g.,
[
11
,
60
,
37
]
for standard treatments of regular variation.
In this subsection, we review the
\mathcal{MHRV}
formalism, a notion of multivariate hidden regular variation proposed in
[
12
]
.
As shown in Section
2.2
,
\mathcal{MHRV}

provides the appropriate framework to describe the tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters.
In particular,
\mathcal{MHRV}

is characterized through the following key elements.

•

Basis
:
\bar{\textbf{S}}=\{\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}\in[0,\infty)^{d}:j\in[k]\}
is a collection of
k
linearly independent vectors;

•

Tail indices
:

\bm{\alpha}=\big\{\alpha(\bm{j})\in[0,\infty):\ \bm{j}\subseteq[k]\big\}

are strictly monotone w.r.t.
\bm{j}
: that is,

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{j})<\alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\bm{j}\subsetneq\bm{j}^{\prime}\subseteq[k],

(2.1)

with the convention that
\alpha(\emptyset)=0
;

•

Rate functions
:
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-\alpha(\bm{j})}(n)
for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
;

•

Limiting measures
:
\big(\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)\big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}
is a collection of Borel measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
.

The
\mathcal{MHRV}
formalism describes how the power-law tail behavior of a Borel measure
\nu
varies across different directions of the Euclidean space.
More precisely,
let

\mathfrak{N}^{d}_{+}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}:\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert=1\}
be the unit sphere under the
L_{1}
norm, restricted to the positive quadrant,
and let (for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j};\bar{\textbf{S}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i\in\bm{j}}w_{i}\bm{\bar{s}}_{i}:\ w_{i}\geq 0\ \forall i\in\bm{j}\Bigg\};

(2.2)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon;\bar{\textbf{S}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bigg\{w\bm{s}:\ w\geq 0,\ \bm{s}\in\mathfrak{N}^{d}_{+},\ \inf_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j};\bar{\textbf{S}})\cap\mathfrak{N}^{d}_{+}}\left\lVert\bm{s}-\bm{x}\right\rVert\leq\epsilon\bigg\},\quad\epsilon>0;

(2.3)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon;\bar{\textbf{S}},\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bigcup_{\bm{j}^{\prime}\subseteq[k]:\ \bm{j}^{\prime}\neq\bm{j},\ \alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})}\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j}^{\prime},\epsilon;\bar{\textbf{S}}),\quad\epsilon>0.

(2.4)

When there is no ambiguity about the choice of
\bar{\textbf{S}}
and
\bm{\alpha}
,
we simplify the notations by writing

\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}{\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j};\bar{\textbf{S}})},

\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon;\bar{\textbf{S}}),

and

\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}{\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon;\bar{\textbf{S}},\bm{\alpha})}.

We also adopt the convention that
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\emptyset,\epsilon)=\{\bm{0}\}
.
Note that
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
is the convex cone generated by
\{\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}:\ i\in\bm{j}\}
,
and

\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
is an enlarged version of
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
by applying an
\epsilon
-perturbation to the angle of each element under the polar transform.
As shown in Definition
2.1
, the
\mathcal{MHRV}
formalism characterizes the heavy tails in a Borel measure
\nu(\cdot)
over each cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
by establishing that, under the
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)
-scaling,
the tail behavior of
\nu\big(\ \cdot\ \cap\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})\big)
converges to the limiting measure
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}
.
In other words, the measure
\nu
exhibits different degrees of hidden regular variation over each cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
,
characterized by the power-law tail index
\alpha(\bm{j})
, the rate function
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
, and the limiting measure
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
.
Here,
we say that
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
is bounded away from
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
if
\inf_{\bm{x}\in A,\ \bm{y}\in B}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\rVert>0
.

Definition 2.1

(
\mathcal{MHRV}
)
.

Let
\nu(\cdot)
be a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
and
\nu_{n}(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\nu(n\ \cdot\ )
(i.e.,
\nu_{n}(A)=\nu(nA)=\nu\big\{n\bm{x}:\ \bm{x}\in A\big\}
).
The measure

\nu(\cdot)
is said to be
multivariate regularly varying

with basis
\bar{\textbf{S}}=\{\bar{s}_{j}:\ j\in[k]\}
,
tail indices
\bm{\alpha}=\big\{\alpha(\bm{j})\in[0,\infty):\ \bm{j}\subseteq[k]\big\}
,
rate functions
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
,
and limiting measures
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
,
denoted as

\nu\in\mathcal{MHRV}\Big(\bar{\textbf{S}},\bm{\alpha},(\lambda_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}},(\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}\Big),

if the asymptotics

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(A^{\circ})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\nu_{n}(A)}{\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\nu_{n}(A)}{\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)}\leq\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(A^{-})<\infty

(2.5)

hold for any
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
and any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
that is
bounded away from
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
under some
\epsilon>0
.
Furthermore, suppose that for any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}

bounded away from
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}([k],\epsilon)
under some
\epsilon>0
,
we also have

\displaystyle\nu_{n}(A)=\mathit{o}(n^{-\gamma})\ \text{ as }n\to\infty,\qquad\forall\gamma>0.

(2.6)

Then we write

\nu\in\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}\Big(\bar{\textbf{S}},\bm{\alpha},(\lambda_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}},(\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}\Big).

We conclude this subsection by briefly noting that:
(i) Conditions (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) are equivalent to characterizations of heavy tails in terms of the
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
-convergence (
[
53
]
) of polar coordinates;
and
(ii)
\mathcal{MHRV}
provides an appropriate framework for describing heavy tails in contexts such as branching processes and Hawkes processes; in particular, the tail asymptotics stated in Section
2.2
would fail under other formalisms of multivariate hidden regular variation (see Remark 7 of
[
12
]
).

2.2
Tail Asymptotics of Hawkes Process Clusters

The cluster representation of Hawkes processes
(see, e.g.,
[
42
,
54
,
26
]
;
see also Definition
E.4
in the Appendix)
reveals that the size of the cluster induced by
a type-
j
immigrant in the Hawkes process
\bm{N}(t)
admits the law of the random vector
\bm{S}_{j}
that solves the distributional fixed-point equation in (
1.2
).
The canonical representation of
\bm{S}_{j}
is the total progeny (across the
d
dimensions) of a multi-type branching process,
where the offspring distributions admit the law
(by Poisson
(X)
for a non-negative variable
X
, we mean the law of

\mathbf{P}\big(\text{Poisson}(X)>y\big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\text{Poisson}(x)>y\big)\mathbf{P}(X\in dx)
)

\displaystyle(B_{i\leftarrow j})_{i\in[d]}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.01042pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$}}\hskip-3.01042pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\Big(\text{Poisson}(\tilde{B}_{1\leftarrow j}\mu^{\bm{N}}_{1\leftarrow j}),\ldots,\text{Poisson}(\tilde{B}_{d\leftarrow j}\mu^{\bm{N}}_{d\leftarrow j})\Big),

(2.7)

where
\big(\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j})_{i\in[d]}
is introduced in Definition
1.1
,
and

\displaystyle\mu^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\left\lVert f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}\right\rVert_{1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(t)dt<\infty,\qquad\forall i,j\in[d],

(2.8)

with
f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(\cdot)
being the decay functions in Definition
1.1
.

In this subsection,
we specialize the assumptions and results in

[
12
]
to our context of Hawkes processes clusters,
which are pivotal to
our large-deviation analysis for multivariate heavy-tailed Hawkes processes.
Specifically,
let

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{i\leftarrow j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\mathbf{E}B_{i\leftarrow j}=\mathbf{E}\big[\tilde{B}_{i\leftarrow j}\big]\cdot\mu^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j},

(2.9)

be the expectation of offspring distributions,
where the equality follows from (
2.7
).
Under Assumption
1
, Proposition 1 of
[
2
]
establishes the existence and uniqueness of the solutions
\bm{S}_{j}
to (
1.2
) and that
\mathbf{E}\left\lVert\bm{S}_{j}\right\rVert<\infty
for all
j\in[d]
.

Assumption 1

(Sub-Criticality)
.

The spectral radius of the mean offspring matrix
\bar{\textbf{B}}=(\bar{b}_{i\leftarrow j})_{j,i\in[d]}

is strictly less than
1
.

Assumption
2
specifies the regularly varying heavy tails in the offspring distribution.
Note that, under the law of the offspring distributions stated in (
2.7
),

B_{j\leftarrow i}
and
\tilde{B}_{j\leftarrow i}
share the same regular variation index
-\alpha_{j\leftarrow i}
.

Assumption 2

(Heavy Tails in Mutual Excitation Rates)
.

For any
(i,j)\in[d]^{2}
,
there exists
\alpha_{j\leftarrow i}\in(1,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\tilde{B}_{j\leftarrow i}>x)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-{\alpha_{j\leftarrow i}}}(x),\qquad\text{as }x\to\infty.

We also impose the following two regularity conditions.

Assumption 3

(Full Connectivity)
.

For any
(i,j)\in[d]^{2}
,
\mathbf{E}S_{j\leftarrow i}>0
.

Assumption 4

(Distinct Tail Indices)
.

In Assumption
2
,

\alpha_{j\leftarrow i}\neq\alpha_{j^{\prime}\leftarrow i^{\prime}}
for any
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in[d]^{2}
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

Theorem
2.2
allows us to characterize
the tail asymptotics of the cluster size vector
\bm{S}_{j}
in terms of
\mathcal{MHRV}
introduced in Definition
2.1
.
To state the results,
we specify the basis, tail indices, rate functions, and limiting measures.
In particular, let the
basis

\bar{\textbf{S}}=\{\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}:\ i\in[d]\}
be

\displaystyle\bar{s}_{j\leftarrow i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\mathbf{E}S_{j\leftarrow i},\qquad\bm{\bar{s}}_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\mathbf{E}\bm{S}_{i}=(\bar{s}_{1\leftarrow i},\ \bar{s}_{2\leftarrow i},\ldots,\bar{s}_{d\leftarrow i})^{\top}.

(2.10)

Assumption
1
ensures that the
\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}
’s are linearly independent.
Next, let

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(j)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\min_{l\in[d]}\alpha_{j\leftarrow l},\qquad l^{*}(j)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\arg\min_{l\in[d]}\alpha_{j\leftarrow l}.

(2.11)

By Assumptions
2
and
4
,
for each
j\in[d]

the argument minimum
l^{*}(j)
uniquely exists and
\alpha^{*}(j)>1
.
Recall that
{\mathcal{P}}_{d}

is the collection of all non-empty subsets of
[d]
.
The
tail indices
are defined by

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}1+\sum_{i\in\bm{j}}\big(\alpha^{*}(i)-1\big),\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}.

(2.12)

As in Section
2.1
,
we adopt the convention
\alpha(\emptyset)=0
.
The
rate functions

are defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}n^{-1}\prod_{i\in\bm{j}}n\mathbf{P}(B_{i\leftarrow l^{*}(i)}>n),\qquad\forall n\geq 1,\ \bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}.

(2.13)

Note that
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-\alpha(\bm{j})}(n)
.
Lastly, for each
i\in[d]
and
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
,
the limiting measure
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot)
is supported on the cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
defined in (
2.2
),
and takes the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\int_{w_{j}\geq 0\ \forall j\in\bm{j}}\mathbbm{I}\Bigg\{\sum_{j\in\bm{j}}w_{j}\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}\in\ \cdot\ \Bigg\}\cdot g^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\bm{w})\bigtimes_{j\in\bm{j}}\frac{dw_{j}}{(w_{j})^{\alpha^{*}(j)+1}},

(2.14)

where we write
\bm{w}=(w_{j})_{j\in\bm{j}}
.
The explicit forms of the functions
g^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\bm{w})
and the limiting measures
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot)
are not needed for stating the main results in Section
3
, so we defer the details to Section
B
in the Appendix. We now state the tail asymptotics of the Hawkes cluster sizes
\bm{S}_{i}
in terms of
\mathcal{MHRV}
.

Theorem 2.2

(Theorem 3.2 of
[
12
]
)
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
4
,
it holds for any
i\in[d]
that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\bm{S}_{i}\in\ \cdot\ )\in\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}\Bigg((\bar{\bm{s}}_{j})_{j\in[d]},\ \big(\alpha(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\subseteq[d]},\ (\lambda_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}},\ \big(\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}\big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\Bigg).

That is,
given
i\in[d]
and
\bm{j}\subseteq[d]
with
\bm{j}\neq\emptyset
, if a Borel measurable set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
is bounded away from
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
under some
\epsilon>0
,
then

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(A^{\circ})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in A)}{\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in A)}{\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)}\leq\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(A^{-})<\infty.

(2.15)

Here,
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
is defined in (
2.3
),
the rate functions
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
are defined in (
2.13
),
and the measures
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
are defined in (
2.14
).
Furthermore, for any Borel measurable set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
that is bounded away from
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\{1,2,\ldots,d\},\epsilon)
under some
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{\gamma}\cdot\mathbf{P}(n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in A)=0,\qquad\forall\gamma>0.

(2.16)

To conclude, we note that the rare events
\{n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in A\}
are driven by multiple big jumps (i.e., multiple nodes giving birth to a disproportionately large number of children) that exhibit sophisticated spatiotemporal structures in the underlying branching processes.
In particular, the contributions of these big jumps align with the vectors
(\bar{\bm{s}}_{l})_{l\in[d]}
,
and, given a sufficiently general set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
,
the exact asymptotics of
\mathbf{P}(n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}\in A)
are dictated by the most likely combination of big jumps that can push
n^{-1}\bm{S}_{i}
into the set
A
, which corresponds to the index set
\bm{j}\subseteq[d]
leading to non-trivial bounds in (
2.15
).
For further details, see also Remark 3 of
[
12
]
.

3
Sample Path Large Deviations

This section presents the main results of this paper and is structured as follows.
Section
3.1
establishes sample path LDPs for Lévy processes with
\mathcal{MHRV}
increments.
Building upon this framework, Section
3.2
then
establishes sample path LDPs for the multivariate Hawkes process

\bm{N}(t)=(N_{1}(t),N_{2}(t),\ldots,N_{d}(t))^{\top}

under the presence of power-law heavy tails in the mutual excitation rates.
Section
3.3
outlines the proof for sample path LDPs of heavy-tailed Hawkes processes.
We defer the underlying proofs to the Appendix.

3.1
Lévy Processes with
\mathcal{MHRV}
Increments

We begin by briefly reviewing the law of a
d
-dimensional Lévy process
\bm{L}=\{\bm{L}(t):\ t\geq 0\}
,
which
is fully characterized by its generating triplet
(\bm{c}_{\bm{L}},\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{L}},\nu)
. Here,

•

\bm{c}_{\bm{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the constant drift of the process,

•

the positive semi-definite matrix
\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
represents the covariance of the Brownian motion component in
\bm{L}(t)
,

•

and the Lévy measure
\nu
is a Borel measure supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
that characterizes the intensity of jumps in
\bm{L}(t)
, and satisfies
\int(\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert^{2}\wedge 1)\nu(d\bm{x})<\infty
.

More precisely, the Lévy process
\bm{L}(t)
admits the Lévy–Itô decomposition

\displaystyle\bm{L}(t)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.01042pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$}}\hskip-3.01042pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bm{c}_{\bm{L}}t+\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{L}}^{1/2}\bm{B}(t)+\int_{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\leq 1}\bm{x}\big[\text{PRM}_{\nu}([0,t]\times d\bm{x})-t\nu(d\bm{x})\big]+\int_{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>1}\bm{x}\text{PRM}_{\nu}([0,t]\times d\bm{x}),

(3.1)

where
\bm{B}
is a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and
\text{PRM}_{\nu}
is a Poisson random measure with intensity measure
\mathcal{L}_{(0,\infty)}\times\nu
and is independent of
\bm{B}
.
Here,
\mathcal{L}_{I}
is the Lebesgue measure restricted on the interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.
We refer the reader to, e.g.,
[
66
]
for a standard treatment of Lévy processes.

The goal of this subsection is to establish Theorem
3.2
and characterize the sample path LDPs for Lévy processes exhibiting multivariate hidden regular variation in the increments.
Beyond its application for sample path LDPs of Hawkes processes that will be explored in Section
3.2
,
the theoretical framework developed in Theorem
3.2
is also of independent interest due to the broad relevance of Lévy processes and hidden regular variation.
Specifically, we
focus on the case where the Lévy measure
\nu
is supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
,
and
characterize the hidden regular variation in
\nu(\cdot)
via the notion of
\mathcal{MHRV}
(see Definition
2.1
) by imposing the following assumption.

Assumption 5

(
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
increments)
.

The Lévy measure
\nu
of
\bm{L}(t)
is supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}=[0,\infty)^{d}
and satisfies

\nu\in\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}\Big((\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})_{i\in[d]},\big(\alpha(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\subseteq[d]},(\lambda_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}},(\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\Big)

under

•

a collection of vectors
(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})_{i\in[d]}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
that are linearly independent,

•

a collection of real numbers
(\alpha(\bm{j}))_{\bm{j}\subseteq[d]}
that are strictly monotone w.r.t.
\bm{j}
(see (
2.1
)) with
\alpha(\emptyset)=0
,

\alpha(\bm{j})>1\ \forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
, and
\alpha(\bm{j})\neq\alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\ \forall\bm{j},\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
with
\bm{j}\neq\bm{j}^{\prime}
,

•

regularly varying functions
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-\alpha(\bm{j})}(n)
(for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
),

•

Borel measures
(\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\subseteq[d]}
where, for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
,
the measure
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}
is
supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
,
such that

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(A)<\infty

holds for any
\epsilon>0
and Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
bounded away from
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
(see (
2.4
)).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)=\prod_{i\in\bm{j}}n\lambda_{\{i\}}(n),\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}.

(3.2)

We stress that both Assumption
5
and Theorem
3.2
are
tailored for the application to heavy-tailed Hawkes processes
.
For instance, the condition (
3.2
) on
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)

is met by (
2.13
)—the rate functions for the
\mathcal{MHRV}
tail of Hawkes process clusters.
Similarly,
an implication of (
3.2
) is that

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{j})-1=\sum_{i\in\bm{j}}\big(\alpha(\{i\})-1\big),\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d},

(3.3)

due to
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-\alpha(\bm{j})}(n)
for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
in
\mathcal{MHRV}
.
Again, this matches (
2.12
)—the tail indices for the
\mathcal{MHRV}
tail of Hawkes process clusters.
Moreover, instead of
\mathcal{MHRV}
, Assumption
5
imposes the stronger
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
condition (see condition (
2.6
) in Definition
2.1
), which agrees with the statements in Theorem
2.2
for the tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters.
For Lévy processes in general contexts,
a relaxed form of Assumption
5
may be considered, which yields a correspondingly weaker version of Theorem
3.2
.
A detailed discussion of this relaxation is provided in Remark
4
.

Throughout the rest of this paper, we use
\mathbb{D}[0,T]=\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})

to denote the space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued càdlàg functions with compact domain
[0,T]
,
and

\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)=\mathbb{D}\big([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued càdlàg functions with unbounded domain
[0,\infty)
.
For the Lévy process
\bm{L}(t)
, let

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\mathbf{E}\bm{L}(1)=\bm{c}_{\bm{L}}+\int_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>1}\bm{x}\nu(d\bm{x}),

(3.4)

where
\bm{c}_{\bm{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the constant drift in the generating triplet of
\bm{L}(t)
.
Let

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bm{L}(nt)/n,\qquad\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t):\ t\in[0,T]\big\}.

(3.5)

The random element
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}

is the scaled version of the sample path of
\bm{L}(t)
embedded in
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
.
Besides,
recall that we adopt notations
\bm{k}=(k_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\in A^{\mathcal{I}}

for vectors of length
|\mathcal{I}|
with each coordinate taking values in
A
and indexed by elements in
\mathcal{I}
.
Informally speaking,
Theorem
3.2
establishes LDP of the form (as
n\to\infty
)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\bigg(\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,T]}}\in\ \cdot\ \cap\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]\bigg)\sim\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{[0,T]}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}(\cdot)\times\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\Big(n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\Big)^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}},\qquad\forall\bm{\mathcal{K}}=\big(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}.

(3.6)

Here, the
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
’s are the rate functions for the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
condition in Assumption
5
,
the vector
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=\big(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
corresponds to a
configuration of big jumps
in the Lévy process,
and the set
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]
contains all paths representing
the typical behavior of
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,T]}}
perturbed by big jumps of the
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
-configuration
.
Therefore, by (
3.6
), the probability of observing a rare event in
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,T]}}
driven by large jumps of the
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
-configuration is of order

\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\big(n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\big)^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}};

furthermore, under this scaling, the leading coefficient of the rare-event probabilities is captured by the limiting measure
\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{[0,T]}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}(\cdot)
.

To formally present Theorem
3.2
,
we introduce the rigorous definitions of the notions involved in (
3.6
).
Given

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\epsilon\geq 0
,
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
, and an interval
I
that is either of the form
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty)
,
let
\bm{1}=\{\bm{1}(t)=t:\ t\in I\}
be the linear function with slope
1
, and define

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(I)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Bigg\{\bm{x}\bm{1}+\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}}\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},\infty)\cap I}:

(3.7)

\displaystyle t_{\bm{j},k}\in I\setminus\{0\}\text{ and }\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon)\ \forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d},\ k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}];\ t_{\bm{j},k}\neq t_{\bm{j}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\ \forall(\bm{j},k)\neq(\bm{j}^{\prime},k^{\prime})\Bigg\}.

In other words,
{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(I)}

is the collection of piece-wise linear functions
\xi
of the form

\displaystyle\xi(t)=\bm{x}t+\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}}\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},\infty)\cap I}(t),\qquad\forall t\in I,

(3.8)

where
each
\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}
belongs to the cone
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
(see (
2.3
)),
and the jump times
(t_{\bm{j},k})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d},\ k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}]}

do not coincide
with one another.
We note that in (
3.8
),
the path
\xi
vanishes at the origin (i.e.,
\xi(0)=\bm{0}
) and, for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
, the path
\xi
has at most
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}
jumps (i.e., discontinuities) whose directions lie in the cone
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
.
Besides, under
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=(0,0,\ldots,0)
, note that
{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{0};\bm{x}}(I)}
contains only the path
\xi(t)=t\bm{x}
.
Meanwhile, since we only consider
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty)
in (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) throughout this paper,
we have either
\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},\infty)\cap I}=\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},T]}
or
\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},\infty)\cap I}=\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},\infty)}
.
Given the tail indices
\big(\alpha(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\subseteq[d]}
in Assumption
5
,
let

\displaystyle\breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}\cdot\Big(\alpha(\bm{j})-1\Big),\qquad\forall\bm{\mathcal{K}}=(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}.

(3.9)

Due to
\alpha(\bm{j})>1\ \forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
(see Assumption
5
),
we have
\breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})>0
whenever
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\neq\bm{0}
.
Meanwhile, note that in (
3.6
), we have

\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\big(n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\big)^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}}\in\mathcal{RV}_{-\breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})}(n).

Next, given
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
, and an interval
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty)
, we define a Borel measure on
\mathbb{D}(I)
by

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{I}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(\ \cdot\ )

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\frac{1}{\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}!}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\int\mathbbm{I}\Bigg\{\bm{x}\bm{1}+\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\sum_{k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}]}\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{\bm{j},k},\infty)\cap I}\in\ \cdot\ \Bigg\}\bigtimes_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\bigtimes_{k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}]}\bigg((\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\times\mathcal{L}_{I})\Big(d(\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k},t_{\bm{j},k})\Big)\bigg),

where the
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}
’s are the limiting measures
in the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
condition of Assumption
5
,

\mathcal{L}_{I}
is the Lebesgue measure restricted on the interval
I
,
and we use
\nu_{1}\times\nu_{2}
to denote the product measure of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
.
Note that, by definitions in (
3.7
)
and the fact that
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}
is supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
,
the measure

{\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{I}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}}

is supported on
{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{0}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(I)}
.

As will be shown in Theorem
3.2
,
scenarios corresponding to different
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
in (
3.6
) can be merged together if the rates of decay

\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\big(n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\big)^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}}

coincide.
This occurs frequently under the condition (
3.2
) in Assumption
5
.
Therefore, this phenomenon is particularly relevant to our analysis of Hawkes processes, as condition (
3.2
) is tailored to the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
tails of Hawkes process clusters (see (
2.13
)).
Specifically,
let

\displaystyle c(\bm{k})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{i\in[d]}k_{i}\cdot\big(\alpha(\{i\})-1\big),\qquad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\prod_{i\in[d]}\Big(n\lambda_{\{i\}}(n)\Big)^{k_{i}},\qquad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d},

(3.12)

where
\big(\alpha(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
and
\big(\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)\big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
are the tail indices and rate functions for the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
condition in Assumption
5
.
Note that for each
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}
, we have
\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-c(\bm{k})}(n)
.
Moreover, Definition
3.1
captures the correspondence between

\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)
in (
3.12
) and
the rates of decay

\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\big(n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\big)^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}}

in (
3.6
).
Here,
for each non-empty index set
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
,
let
\bm{e}(\bm{j})=\big(e_{1}(\bm{j}),\ldots,e_{d}(\bm{j})\big)^{\top}
,
where
e_{l}(\bm{j})=\mathbbm{I}\{l\in\bm{j}\}
.
That is, in the vector
\bm{e}({\bm{j}})
, each coordinate
e_{l}(\bm{j})
indicates whether
l
belongs to the index set
\bm{j}
or not.

Definition 3.1

(Allocation)
.

Given
\bm{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}
,
the vector
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
is said to be
an allocation of
\bm{k}
if

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}\bm{e}({\bm{j}})=\bm{k}.

(3.13)

We use
\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
to denote the set of all allocations of
\bm{k}
.

Remark 1
.

We note a few important implications of Definition
3.1
.
First,
for each
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}
, there exist only finitely many allocations (i.e.,
|\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})|<\infty
).
Second, by (
3.3
),

\displaystyle\breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})=c(\bm{k}),\qquad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d},\ \bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k}),

(3.14)

where
c(\cdot)
and
\breve{c}(\cdot)
are defined in (
3.11
) and (
3.9
), respectively.
Similarly, by (
3.2
),

\displaystyle\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)=\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\Big(n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\Big)^{\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}},\qquad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\},\ \bm{\mathcal{K}}=(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k}).

(3.15)

Furthermore,
given
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}
, and an interval
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty)
, let

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\Bigg\{\bm{x}\bm{1}+\sum_{j\in[d]}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{j}}w_{j,k}\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}\mathbbm{I}_{[t_{k,j},\infty)\cap I}:

(3.16)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad t_{j,k}\in I\setminus\{0\}\text{ and }w_{j,k}\geq 0\ \forall j\in[d],\ k\in[k_{j}]\Bigg\}

be the collection of all piece-wise linear functions that have slope
\bm{x}
and make jumps along the vectors
(\bar{\bm{s}}_{j})_{j\in[d]}
.
Note that in (
3.16
), we allow the
t_{j,k}
’s—the arrival times of jumps—to coincide,
which further
allows the paths in
\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)
to exhibit discontinuities that lie in the cones
\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
(see (
2.2
)).
Therefore,
by definitions in (
3.16
) and (
3.7
),
we have

{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{0}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(I)}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)

for any
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}
and
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
.
As a result, given
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}
and
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
, the support of

{\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{I}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}}

defined in (
3.10
)
is a subset of
\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)
.

Now, we are ready to state Theorem
3.2
.
Given
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\epsilon\geq 0
,
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
, and interval
I
of the form
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty)
,
we define

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}:\\
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\notin\mathbb{A}(\bm{k}),\ \breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})\leq c(\bm{k})\end{subarray}}\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(I).

(3.17)

For the càdlàg space
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
, recall the definition of the Skorokhod
J_{1}
metric

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(x,y)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\inf_{\lambda\in\Lambda[0,T]}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\lambda(t)-t|\vee\left\lVert x(\lambda(t))-y(t)\right\rVert,\qquad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{D}[0,T],

(3.18)

where

\Lambda[0,T]

is the set of all homeomorphisms on
[0,T]
.
Theorem
3.2
establishes sample path LDPs for Lévy processes with
\mathcal{MHRV}
increments
w.r.t. the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let Assumption
5
hold.
Let
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
,
and let
B
be a Borel set of
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
equipped with the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology.
Suppose that
B

is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]
under
\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}
for some
\epsilon>0
.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(B^{\circ})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}\in B)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}\in B)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)}\leq\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(B^{-})<\infty,

(3.19)

where
\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
is the set containing all allocations of
\bm{k}
(see Definition
3.1
).

We provide the detailed proof of Theorem
3.2
in Section
D
of the Appendix,
and briefly note that the limiting measures of the form

\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}^{I}(\cdot)

can be efficiently evaluated via Monte Carlo simulation in the context of Hawkes processes
(see Remark
6
of Section
3.2
).
To conclude this subsection, we add a few remarks about
the interpretation of the LDP (
3.19
),
its proof strategy,
and relaxations of assumptions in Theorem
3.2
.

Remark 2

(Interpretations of Asymptotics (
3.19
))
.

Theorem
3.2
shows that, given the rare event set
B
,
the asymptotics of the form (
3.19
) hold for any
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}

such that
B
is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]
under
\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}
for some
\epsilon>0
.
However, the choice of
\bm{k}
that attains non-trivial bounds in (
3.19
) is determined by
(with
\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]
defined in (
3.16
))

\displaystyle\bm{k}(B)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}:\ B\cap\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}c(\bm{k}).

(3.20)

Indeed, as noted in Remark
1
,
the measure

{\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}}(\cdot)

is supported on

\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]

with
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
.
To attain a strictly positive upper bound in (
3.19
),
we must have

B\cap\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]\neq\emptyset.

Therefore,
given any Borel set
B\subset\mathbb{D}[0,T]
that does not contain the linear path with slope
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}
,
the LDP (
3.19
) implies

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{k}(B)}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(B^{\circ})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}\in B)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}(B)}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}\in B)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}(B)}(n)}\leq{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{k}(B)}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(B^{-})

with limiting measure

{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{k}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}=\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}^{{}_{[0,T]}},

provided that
\bm{k}(B)
uniquely exists and
B
is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k}(B);\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}}[0,T]
under
\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}
for some
\epsilon>0
.
From this perspective, it is worth noting that: (i)
\bm{k}(B)
plays a role analogous to that of rate functions in the classical LDP; and (ii)
the power-law rates of decay of rare-event probabilities
\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}\in B)
, as well as limiting behavior of
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}
conditioned on such rare events,
are determined by the discrete optimization problem in (
3.20
) regarding
the likelihood of entering
the set
B
.
In particular, note that
the nominal behavior of the scaled path
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}
is the linear function with slope
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}
.
Meanwhile,
by
Definition
2.1
and
the
\mathcal{MHRV}
condition in Assumption
5
,
the probability of observing a big jump in
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}
that is aligned with the cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})

(equivalently, observing a jump in
\bm{L}(t)
that is of the size
\mathcal{O}(n)
and lies in the cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})
, over the time horizon
t\in[0,Tn]
)
is of order
T\cdot n\lambda_{\bm{j}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{1-\alpha(\bm{j})}(n)
.
Therefore,
the function
\breve{c}(\cdot)
in (
3.9
) (and hence
c(\cdot)
in (
3.11
) by the equality (
3.14
))
corresponds to the “rareness” of observing a certain configuration of big jumps in the Lévy process,
and
the solution
\bm{k}(B)
in (
3.20
)
corresponds to
the most likely configuration of big jumps
that can push the linear path
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}\mathbf{1}
—the nominal path of
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}
—into the rare event set
B
.

Remark 3

(Proof Sketch for Theorem
3.2
)
.

Our proof of Theorem
3.2
relies on the notion of asymptotic equivalence in terms of
\mathbb{M}
-convergence (
[
53
]
).
First,
Proposition
D.2
shows that,
for the purpose of proving (
3.19
),
it suffices to
study the large-jump approximation
\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}
, which removes any discontinuity in
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}_{n}
with norm less than
\delta
.
The key tool is Lemma
D.4
, which develops concentration inequalities for the Lévy process
\bm{L}(t)
stopped right before the arrival of the first large jump.
Next, Proposition
D.3
provides asymptotic analyses of
\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}
.
In particular,
Lemma
D.6
establishes the asymptotic law of the arrival times and sizes of large jumps in
\bm{L}(t)
.
We achieve this by differentiating the large jumps based on their directions and associating them with one of the cones
\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
,
which enables the application of the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
tail condition in Assumption
5
.
Then, we obtain Proposition
D.3
through a continuous mapping argument.

Remark 4

(Relaxation of Assumptions)
.

As noted earlier,
the condition (
3.2
) on the rate functions and
the

\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
tail condition (instead of
\mathcal{MHRV}
) in Assumption
5

are specifically designed for the subsequent analysis of Hawkes processes. In more general settings, one may adopt a more flexible—though less detailed—description of multivariate hidden regular variation in the Lévy measure
\nu(\cdot)
.
In Section
F
of the Appendix, we present an analog of Theorem
3.2
for such settings, where condition (
3.2
) is dropped, a weaker
\mathcal{MHRV}
condition is used, and the number of vectors in the basis is not fixed at
d
.
Furthermore, our proof strategy for Theorem
3.2
could be extended to Lévy measures supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
rather than just
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
, or to settings where the tail behavior is described using alternative formalisms such as Adapted-MRV (see
[
27
]
).
We do not pursue these extensions here, as they are not closely related to our focus on Hawkes processes in this paper.

3.2
Multivariate Heavy-Tailed Hawkes Processes

In this subsection, we
study sample path LDPs for multivariate heavy-tailed Hawkes processes w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)=\mathbb{D}\big([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
.
Here,
the product
M_{1}
topology is induced by projecting any path
\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)

onto the
d
-fold product space of
\mathbb{D}\big([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}\big)
,
and then taking the maximum of the Skorokhod
M_{1}
metric along the
d
dimensions over this product space.

To be more specific, we first review the Skorokhod
M_{1}
metric of the càdlàg space with codomain
\mathbb{R}
.
For any path
\xi\in\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R})
, let

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\xi}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Big\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]:\ z\in[\xi(t-)\wedge\xi(t),\xi(t-)\vee\xi(t)]\Big\}

be the connected graph of
\xi
, where we take
\xi(0-)=\xi(0)
.
We define an order over the connected graph
\Gamma_{\xi}
by saying
(z_{1},t_{1})\leq(z_{2},t_{2})
if
(i)
t_{1}<t_{2}
or
(ii)
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
|z_{1}-\xi(t_{1}-)|\leq|z_{2}-\xi(t_{2}-)|
.
A mapping
(u(\cdot),s(\cdot))
is said to be a parametric representation of
\xi
if

t\mapsto(u(t),s(t))
is a continuous and non-decreasing (over
\Gamma_{\xi}
) mapping
such that
\{(u(t),s(t)):\ t\in[0,1]\}=\Gamma_{\xi}
.
The Skorokhod
M_{1}
metric on
\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R}\big)
is defined by (for any
\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R}\big)
)

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{M_{1}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u_{i},s_{i})\in\Pi(\xi^{(i)})\\
i=1,2\end{subarray}}\ \sup_{t\in[0,1]}|u_{1}(t)-u_{2}(t)|\vee|s_{1}(t)-s_{2}(t)|,

(3.21)

where
\Pi(\xi)
is the set of all parametric representations of
\xi
.
In the multivariate setting,
the product
M_{1}
metric of
\mathbb{D}[0,T]=\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
is defined by

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\max_{j\in[d]}\bm{d}_{M_{1}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}(\xi^{(1)}_{j},\xi^{(2)}_{j}),\qquad\forall\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{D}[0,T],

(3.22)

where we write
\xi^{(i)}=(\xi^{(i)}_{1},\xi^{(i)}_{2},\ldots,\xi^{(i)}_{d})^{\top}
,
and the subscript
\mathcal{P}
indicates the uniform metric on the product space.
Given
t\in(0,\infty)
, we define the projection mapping
\phi_{t}:\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{D}[0,t]
by

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(\xi)(s)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\xi(s),\qquad\forall s\in[0,t].

(3.23)

This allows us to define the product
M_{1}
metric on
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
:

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\cdot\Big[\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,t]}}\Big(\phi_{t}\big(\xi^{(1)}),\ \phi_{t}\big(\xi^{(2)}\big)\Big)\wedge 1\Big]dt,\qquad\forall\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty).

(3.24)

We note that
the topology induced by the product metric
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,T]}}
agrees with the
weak
M_{1}
topology of the càdlàg space
(see, e.g., Chapter 12 of
[
73
]
).

Moving on to the large deviations analysis for multivariate Hawkes processes,
we work with the heavy-tailed setting in Section
2.2

and impose
Assumptions
1
–
4
.
Besides,
we adopt the definitions of
(\bar{\bm{s}}_{j})_{j\in[d]}
,
\alpha(\cdot)
,
\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
, and

{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\bm{j}}(\cdot)}
in
(
2.10
)–(
2.14
),
which specify the basis, tail indices, rate functions, and limiting measures for the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
tail of Hawkes process clusters established in Theorem
2.2
.
Throughout this subsection, we set

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{i\in[d]}c^{\bm{N}}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot),\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d},

(3.25)

where the constants

c^{\bm{N}}_{i}
are the immigration rates of the Hawkes process (see (
1.1
)).
Next,
let

\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)
,

c(\bm{k})
,
\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(\cdot)
,

\breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})
,

{\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{I}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{x}}(\ \cdot\ )},

and

{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{x}}(I)}

be defined as in (
3.7
)–(
3.17
) of Section
3.1
,
with
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}
specified as in (
3.25
).
We are ready to state Theorem
3.3
, the main result of this section.
For each
n\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}(t)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bm{N}(nt)/n,\qquad\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}(t):\ t\in[0,\infty)\big\},

(3.26)

with
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}

being the scaled version of the sample path of
\bm{N}(t)
embedded in
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
.
Besides, let

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{i\in[d]}c^{\bm{N}}_{i}\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},

(3.27)

where the
c^{\bm{N}}_{i}
’s are the immigration rates of the Hawkes process in (
1.1
).
Under the presence of regularly varying tails in the offspring distributions,
Theorem
3.3
develops sample path LDPs for
\bm{N}(t)
w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let Assumptions
1
–
4
hold.
Let

\bm{k}=(k_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}

and let
B
be a Borel set in
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
equipped with the product
M_{1}
topology.
Suppose that
B
is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
under
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}
for some
\epsilon>0
, and

\displaystyle\int_{x/\log x}^{\infty}f^{\bm{N}}_{p\leftarrow q}(t)dt=\mathit{o}\big(\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(x)\big/x\big)\ \text{ as }x\to\infty,\qquad\forall(p,q)\in[d]^{2},

(3.28)

where the
f^{\bm{N}}_{p\leftarrow q}
’s are the decay functions in (
1.1
),
and the
\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{j}}(n)
’s are defined in (
3.12
).
Besides, suppose that
\alpha(\bm{j})\neq\alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})
for any
\bm{j},\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
with
\bm{j}\neq\bm{j}^{\prime}
,
where
\alpha(\cdot)
is defined in (
2.12
).
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B^{\circ})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)}\leq\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B^{-})<\infty,

(3.29)

where
\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
is the set containing all allocations of
\bm{k}
(see Definition
3.1
).

The intuition of the sample path LDP (
3.29
) is that,
given any Borel set
B\subseteq\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
that is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
,
the rare-event probabilities (roughly) admit the asymptotics

\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B)\sim\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)\cdot\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B)

as
n\to\infty
,
thus exhibiting a power-law rate of decay
\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-c(\bm{k})}(n)

with leading coefficient
\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B)
.
We elaborate in Remark
5

how the rate functions for
\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B)
are determined via a discrete optimization problem regarding the geometry of
B
and the tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters.

We provide the proof of Theorem
3.3
in Section
3.3
.
To conclude, we explain in Remarks
6
–
8

the evaluation of the limiting measures in (
3.29
) via Monte Carlo simulation,
the topological choices in Theorem
3.3
,
as well as the necessity of condition (
3.28
).

Remark 5

(Interpretations of Asymptotics (
3.29
))
.

Analogous to Remark
2
,
the LDP
(
3.29
) implies

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{k}(B)}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}(B^{\circ})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}(B)}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}(B)}(n)}\leq{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{k}(B)}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}(B^{-})

for sufficiently general Borel sets
B
of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
equipped with the product
M_{1}
topology,
with

\bm{k}(B)=\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}:\ B\cap\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}c(\bm{k}),

the function
c(\cdot)
defined in (
3.11
),
the set
\mathbb{D}_{\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,T]
defined in (
3.16
),
and

{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{k}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}=\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}.

This is made precise by Theorem
3.3
whenever

\bm{k}(B)
uniquely exists and
B
is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k}(B);\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
under
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}
for some
\epsilon>0
.
In other words,
the power-law rates of decay for
\mathbf{P}(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B)
, as well as limiting behavior of
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
conditioned on such rare events,
are dictated by the discrete optimization problem in
\bm{k}(B)
.
In particular, by the tail asymptotics of Hawkes process clusters stated in Theorem
2.2
,
the probability of observing a large cluster (i.e., of size
\mathcal{O}(n)
) whose size vector aligns with the cone
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j})
is roughly of order
n^{-\alpha(\bm{j})}
with
\alpha(\cdot)
defined in (
2.12
).
From this perspective, the function
\breve{c}(\cdot)
in (
3.9
)
(and hence the function
c(\cdot)
in (
3.11
) by the equality (
3.14
))
characterizes the “rareness” of any configuration of large clusters
and
\bm{k}(B)
identifies the
most likely configuration of large clusters
that can push

\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}\mathbf{1}
—the nominal path of the (scaled) Hawkes process
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
—into the rare event set
B
.

Remark 6

(Evaluation of Limiting Measures through Monte Carlo Simulation)
.

We stress that the limiting measures

\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(\cdot)

in (
3.29
) can be evaluated efficiently via Monte Carlo simulation.
Specifically, given a Borel set
B\subseteq\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
3.3
,
