we verify in
Lemma
E.1
the following claims
under any
\bar{\delta}>0
small enough and
T>0
large enough:
for any
\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
,

\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
, and

\xi\in B\cap\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
,
in the expression (
3.8
) for
\xi
we have

t_{\bm{j},k}\leq T,

\left\lVert\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}\right\rVert>\bar{\delta},

and

\bm{w}_{\bm{j},k}\notin\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})

for each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d},\ k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}]
.
Therefore, as long as we can sample from probability measures
(where the
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}
’s are defined in (
3.25
))

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\Big(\ \cdot\ \cap\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\Big)\Big/\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\Big(\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\Big),\quad\text{with }\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta}):\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\bar{\delta}\},

(3.30)

and evaluate the normalization constants
\bar{c}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}=\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\Big(\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\Big)
,
by (
3.10
) we can
run Monte Carlo simulation to estimate
(under
\bar{\delta}>0
sufficiently small and
T>0
sufficiently large)

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B)=\frac{\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}(T\cdot\bar{c}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}})^{|\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}|}}{\prod_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}!}\cdot\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathbbm{I}\bigg\{\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}\mathbf{1}+\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\sum_{k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}]}\bm{Z}^{(\bm{j},k)}\mathbbm{I}_{[T\cdot U^{(\bm{j},k)},\infty)}\in B\bigg\}\Bigg],

(3.31)

where the
U^{(\bm{j},k)}
’s are i.i.d. copies of Unif
(0,1)
, and each
\bm{Z}^{(\bm{j},k)}
is an independent copy under the law
\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}
.
Furthermore, the evaluation of constants
\bar{c}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}
are detailed in Remark 4 of
[
12
]
,
and rejection sampling addresses the generation of

\bm{Z}^{(\bm{j},k)}\sim\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}(\cdot)
,
thanks to the specific form of the limiting measures
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot)
in (
2.14
) for Theorem
2.2
.
We elaborate on the rejection sampling for
\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}(\cdot)
in Section
B
of the Appendix,
where we also provide the explicit expressions for the limiting measures
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot)
in (
2.14
).

Remark 7

(Topological Choices in Theorem
3.3
)
.

Concerning the choice of Skorokhod non-uniform topologies for càdlàg spaces,
we note that the characterization of asymptotics (
3.29
)
under the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
in Theorem
3.3
is, in general, the tightest one can hope for.

•

Compared to the
J_{1}
topology, the
M_{1}
topology would be the right choice for the large deviations analysis of Hawkes processes, as it allows merging jumps that correspond to distinct but relatively close arrival times of all descendants within the same cluster.

•

Suppose that the size vector of a cluster is
\bm{s}
.
At any moment
t
, the current size of the cluster (i.e., containing all descendants born by time
t
) stays within the hypercube
\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}:\ \bm{x}\leq\bm{s}\}

but would generally deviate from the ray
\{w\bm{s}:\ w\geq 0\}
due to the randomness in the birth times.
Such arbitrariness and non-linearity in the growth of cluster sizes prevent the LDP (
3.29
) to hold under the strong
M_{1}
topology and promotes the use of the product (i.e., weak)
M_{1}
topology.

•

Note that this issue regarding the arbitrary shape of truncated clusters can be safely dismissed for univariate Hawkes processes, whose cluster sizes are non-negative scalar variables.
In fact, as detailed in Section
E.4
of the Appendix,
Theorem
3.3

can be strengthened to the
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R}\big)
—the càdlàg space with compact domain—in the univariate setting
.
Compared to the statements in Theorem
3.3
w.r.t.
\big(\mathbb{D}\big([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}\big),\bm{d}_{M_{1}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}\big)
(when applied to univariate cases),
such extensions to
\big(\mathbb{D}\big([0,T],\mathbb{R}\big),\bm{d}_{M_{1}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,T]}}}\big)

allow us to address
a broader class of events and functionals for heavy-tailed Hawkes processes in the univariate setting.

•

In comparison,
as we demonstrate in
Example
1

in Section
C
of the Appendix,
the characterization of LDPs in Theorem
3.3
w.r.t.
the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)

is generally the tightest one can hope for in the multivariate setting
.
The intuition of the counterexample is that, for any immigrant that arrives almost at the end of the time interval
[0,T]
,
its cluster size vector could be truncated arbitrarily at time
T
due to the randomness in the birth times of the descendants, which prevents (
3.29
) to hold w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
for rare events set
B
involving the value of the process at time
T
.
In particular, we note that Example
1
is valid even if the decay functions
f^{\bm{N}}_{i\leftarrow j}(\cdot)
in (
1.1
) have bounded support or exponentially decaying tails.
In other words,
such pathological cases would still arise in
(\mathbb{D}[0,T],\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,T]}}})
even if we strengthen the condition (
3.28
) or impose exponentially decaying bounds on the tail of birth time distributions of the offspring.

Remark 8

(Tail Conditions on Decay Functions in Theorem
3.3
)
.

Even in the univariate setting,
power-law bounds (like (
3.28
)) on the tail behavior of decay functions are required for Theorem
3.3
to hold.
In particular, Example
2
in Section
C
of the Appendix demonstrates that, as long as the decay function does have a power-law tail,
one can always identify a simple class of counterexamples regardless of the actual values of the power-law tail indices in the excitation rates and the decay functions of the Hawkes process.
The intuition is that, without sufficiently tight tail bounds on the decay functions,
some rare events are driven by a large cluster with a long lifetime (i.e., a single big jump spread too widely over the entire timeline), rather than by multiple large clusters.

3.3
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Our proof strategy is to establish the asymptotic equivalence between

\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
, the scaled sample path of the Hawkes processes defined in (
3.26
), and some Lévy process with
\mathcal{MHRV}
increments.
Specifically,
consider the multivariate compound Poisson process

\displaystyle\bm{L}(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{i\in[d]}\sum_{k\geq 0}\bm{S}^{(k)}_{i}\mathbbm{I}_{[T^{\mathcal{C}}_{i;k},\infty)}(t),\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

(3.32)

Here, independently
for each
i\in[d]
,
we use

0<T^{\mathcal{C}}_{i;1}<T^{\mathcal{C}}_{i;2}<\ldots

to denote a sequence generated by a Poisson process with constant rate
c^{\bm{N}}_{i}
introduced in (
1.1
).
Besides, independent from the sequences
(T^{\mathcal{C}}_{i;k})_{i\in[d],k\geq 1}
,
each
\bm{S}^{(k)}_{j}
is an i.i.d. copy of
\bm{S}_{j}
that solves distributional fixed-point equation (
1.2
).
The compound Poisson process
\bm{L}(t)
in (
3.32
) is intimately related to
\bm{N}(t)
through the cluster representation of Hawkes processes,
which we detail in Definition
E.4
of the Appendix
(see also
[
42
,
54
,
26
]
).
In particular, we consider
a coupling between
\bm{N}(t)
and
\bm{L}(t)

such that
T^{\mathcal{C}}_{i;k}
represents the arrival time of the
k^{\text{th}}
type-
i
immigrant in the Hawkes process
\bm{N}(t)
, and
each
\bm{S}^{(k)}_{i}
is size vector of the cluster induced by the
k^{\text{th}}
type-
i
immigrant.
From this perspective, the process
\bm{L}(t)
in (
3.32
) is obtained by collapsing any cluster in
\bm{N}(t)
into a single jump, as if there is no gap between the birth times of the immigrant inducing the cluster and any offspring in this cluster.

More precisely,
our proof of Theorem
3.3
relies on the following two propositions,
whose detailed proofs are provided in Sections
E.2
–
E.3
of the Appendix.
First, by exploiting the
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
tail asymptotics established in Theorem
2.2
for the cluster size vectors
\bm{S}_{i}
,
Proposition
3.4
shows that

\bm{L}(t)
is a Lévy process with
\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}
increments.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let Assumptions
1
–
4
hold.
The process
\bm{L}(t)
defined in (
3.32
)
is a Lévy process with generating triplet
(\bm{0},\textbf{0},\nu)
(i.e., with no linear drift and no Brownian motion component) and the Lévy measure
\nu
takes the form

\displaystyle\nu(\cdot)=\sum_{i\in[d]}c^{\bm{N}}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{P}(\bm{S}_{i}\in\ \cdot\ ),

(3.33)

which implies
\mathbf{E}\bm{L}(1)=\sum_{i\in[d]}c^{\bm{N}}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{E}\bm{S}_{i}=\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}
(see (
3.27
)).
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\nu(\cdot)\in\mathcal{MHRV}^{*}\Bigg((\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})_{i\in[d]},\ \big(\alpha(\bm{j})\big)_{\bm{j}\subseteq[d]},\ (\lambda_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}},\ (\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\Bigg),

(3.34)

where
\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}=\mathbf{E}\bm{S}_{i}
,
and

\alpha(\cdot)
,

\lambda_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)
,

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}(\cdot)

are defined in
(
2.12
),
(
2.13
),
and (
3.25
), respectively.

Proposition
3.4
allows us to apply Theorem
3.2
and obtain sample path LDPs of the form (
3.29
) for the scaled paths of
\bm{L}(t)
, i.e., (under
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bm{L}(nt)/n
),

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,T]}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t):\ t\in[0,T]\big\},\quad\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t):\ t\in[0,\infty)\big\}.

(3.35)

Next, to close the loop for the proof of Theorem
3.3
,
we
show that

\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
and
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
(almost always) stay close under the metric
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}
.
In particular, by refining the approach in
[
61
]
,
under condition (
3.28
) we establish
power-law tail bounds for the lifetime of Hawkes process clusters, i.e., the gap in the birth times between an immigrant and its last offspring.
This leads to Proposition
3.5
and verifies that, when collapsing each cluster in
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
into a single jump and obtaining
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
, the perturbation under
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}
is inconsequential for the purpose of establishing the LDP (
3.29
).

Proposition 3.5
.

Let Assumptions
1
–
4
hold.
Let

\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
.
Under condition (
3.28
),
it holds for any
\Delta>0
that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n},\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\big)>\Delta\Big)\Big/\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)=0,

where
\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(\cdot)
is defined in (
3.12
).

We add two remarks regarding the technical tools involved.
First, for the proof of Theorem
3.3
,
we need to adapt Theorem
3.2
to
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
.
The results are stated in Theorem
E.3
,
and
we detail the steps in Section
E.1
of the Appendix.
Second, the asymptotic equivalence between

\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
and
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}

is stated in terms of the
\mathbb{M}
-convergence (
[
53
]
) over the metric space
\big(\mathbb{D}[0,\infty),\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}\big)
,
and is made precise by our Lemma
A.3
in the Appendix.
In particular,
under the choice of

\displaystyle(\mathbb{S},\bm{d})=\big(\mathbb{D}[0,\infty),\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}\big),\quad\mathbb{C}=\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty),\quad X_{n}=\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}},\quad Y_{n}^{\delta}=\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}\ \forall\delta>0,

and with a dummy marker
V^{\delta}_{n}\equiv 1,\ \mathcal{V}=\{1\}
in
Lemma
A.3
,
to prove
Claim (
3.29
)
it suffices to verify the following two conditions
(given our choice of
Y^{\delta}_{n}
, we also write
Y_{n}=Y^{\delta}_{n}=\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}
):

1.

The claim

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}\big(X_{n},Y_{n}\big)>\Delta\Big)}\Big/\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)=0

holds for each
\Delta>0
and Borel set
B\subseteq\mathbb{S}
that is bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
;

2.

For each Borel set
B\subseteq\mathbb{S}
that is bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
,
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}(Y_{n}\in B)/\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)\leq\mu(B^{-})<\infty

and

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}(Y_{n}\in B)/\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)\geq\mu(B^{\circ}),

where
\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(\cdot)
.

To conclude, we provide the—now succinct—proof of Theorem
3.3
, and refer to Section
G
for the full theorem tree.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

As noted above, by Lemma
A.3
, it suffices to verify Conditions (i) and (ii).
Proposition
3.5
verifies Condition (i), and for Condition (ii) it is equivalent to verifying the following claim:
let

\bm{k}=(k_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
,
and
B
be a Borel set of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
equipped with the product
M_{1}
topology;
if
B
is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
under
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}

for some
\epsilon>0
,
then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B^{\circ})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in B\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)}\leq\sum_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(B^{-})<\infty.

(3.36)

This is almost an immediate consequence of Theorem
3.2
,
except that we need to adapt it to
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
—the space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued càdlàg functions with unbounded domain.
To this end, we apply
Theorem
E.3
—the
\big(\mathbb{D}[0,\infty),\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}\big)
counterpart of Theorem
3.2
—to obtain sample path LDPs of the form (
3.36
) for
\bar{\bm{L}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
.
Here, the metric is defined by

\displaystyle{\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\cdot\Big[\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,t]}}\Big(\phi_{t}\big(\xi^{(1)}),\ \phi_{t}\big(\xi^{(2)}\big)\Big)\wedge 1\Big]dt,\qquad\forall\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty),

with
\phi_{t}(\xi)(s)=\xi(s)
being the projection mapping from
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
to
\mathbb{D}[0,t]
.
More specifically, the finite upper bound in (
3.36
) is verified by Theorem
E.3
.
Besides,
by Theorem
E.3
and Proposition
3.4
,
Claim (
3.36
) holds if
B
is also bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
under
\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}
.
However, due to
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,T]}}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,T]}}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}[0,T]
(i.e., the hierarchy of Skorokhod non-uniform topologies),
we have

\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})

for any
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
,
and hence

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}\Big(B,\ \breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)\Big)\geq\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}\Big(B,\ \breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)\Big)>0.

Here, the strictly positive lower bound holds since, by our running assumption, we take
B
that is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,\infty)
under
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{{}_{[0,\infty)}}}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
-Convergence and Asymptotic Equivalence

In this appendix, we first review the notion of
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
-convergence in
[
53
]
,
which has been a key tool in large deviations analyses of heavy-tailed stochastic systems
[
53
,
63
,
20
,
12
]
.
Next, we establish an asymptotic equivalence result.
Let
(\mathbb{S},\bm{d})
be a complete and separable metric space.
Given Borel measurable sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{S}
,

A
is said to be bounded away from
B
(under
\bm{d}
) if

\bm{d}(A,B)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\inf_{x\in A,y\in B}\bm{d}(x,y)>0.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be the
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{S}
.
Given a Borel measurable subset
\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mathbb{S}
,
let
\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}

be the metric subspace of
\mathbb{S}
in the relative topology with

\sigma
-algebra

\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{A\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{S}}:\ A\subseteq\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}\}.

The space of measures

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{\nu(\cdot)\text{ is a Borel measure on }\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}:\ \nu(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}^{r})<\infty\ \forall r>0\}.

can be topologized by the sub-basis constructed using sets of the form

\{\nu\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}):\ \nu(f)\in G\},

where
G\subseteq[0,\infty)
is open,
f\in\mathcal{C}({\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}})
,
and

\mathcal{C}({\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}})

is the set of all real-valued, non-negative, bounded and continuous functions with support bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
(i.e., there exists
r>0
such that
f(x)=0\ \forall x\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
).
Now, we recall the concept of
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
-convergence.

Definition A.1

(
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
-convergence)
.

Given a sequence
\mu_{n}\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
and some
\mu\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
,
we say that
\mu_{n}
converges to
\mu
in
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
as
n\to\infty

if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}(f)-\mu(f)|=0,\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C}).

When there is no ambiguity about the choice of
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{C}
, we simply refer to the convergence mode in Definition
A.1
as
\mathbb{M}
-convergence.
Next, we review the Portmanteau Theorem for
\mathbb{M}
-convergence.

Theorem A.2

(Theorem 2.1 of
[
53
]
)
.

Let
\mu_{n},\mu\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
.
The following statements are equivalent.

1.

\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
as
n\to\infty
.

2.

\int fd\mu_{n}\rightarrow\int fd\mu
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
that is also uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{S}
.

3.

For any closed set
F
and open set
G
that are bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(F)\leq\mu(F),\qquad\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(G)\geq\mu(G).

The verification of
\mathbb{M}
-convergence
is often facilitated by
the asymptotic equivalence between two families of random objects.
In the proof of this paper, we work with the version of asymptotic equivalence in Lemma
A.3
, which is in the same spirit as
Lemma 4.2 of
[
12
]
with only two key differences.
First,
Lemma
A.3
addresses abstract metric spaces, whereas Lemma 4.2 of
[
12
]
specifically applies to the space of polar coordinates.
Second, we require condition (ii) to hold only for
almost
all
\delta>0
sufficiently close to
0
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
X_{n}
and
Y^{\delta}_{n}
be random elements taking values in a complete and separable metric space
(\mathbb{S},\bm{d})
,
and
V_{n}^{\delta}
be random elements taking values in a countable set
\mathbb{V}
.
Furthermore,
given
n\geq 1
,
X_{n}
,
Y_{n}^{\delta}
, and
V^{\delta}_{n}
(for any
\delta>0
) are supported on the same probability space.
Let
\epsilon_{n}
be a sequence of positive real numbers with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
.
Let
\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mathbb{S}
,
let

\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{V}
be a set containing only finitely many elements,
and let
\mu_{v}\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that

1.

(
Asymptotic equivalence
)
Given
\Delta>0
and
B\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
that is bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}{\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}\big(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n}\big)\mathbbm{I}\big(X_{n}\in B\text{ or }Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B\big)>\Delta\Big)}=0;

2.

(
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
-Convergence
)
Given
B\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
that is bounded away from
\mathbb{C}

and
\Delta\in\big(0,\bm{d}(B,\mathbb{C})\big)
,
there exists some
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(B,\Delta)>0

such that for all but countably many
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B,V^{\delta}_{n}=v)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{v}(B^{\Delta}),\ \ \liminf_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B,V^{\delta}_{n}=v)\geq\mu_{v}(B_{\Delta}),\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{V},

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B,\ V^{\delta}_{n}\notin\mathcal{V})=0.

Then

\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in\ \cdot\ )\rightarrow\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(\cdot)
in
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
.

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

Take any Borel measurable
B\subseteq\mathbb{S}
that is bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
.
W.l.o.g., in this proof we only consider
\Delta>0
small enough
that
\bm{d}(B,\mathbb{C})>2\Delta
, and hence
B^{2\Delta}
is still bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
.

First, for any
n\geq 1
and
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\big(X_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})\leq\Delta\big)+\mathbf{P}\big(X_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})>\Delta\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B^{\Delta}\big)+\mathbf{P}\big(X_{n}\in B\text{ or }Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})>\Delta\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B^{\Delta},\ V^{\delta}_{n}=v\big)+\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B^{\Delta},\ V^{\delta}_{n}\notin\mathcal{V}\big)+\mathbf{P}\big(X_{n}\in B\text{ or }Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})>\Delta\big).

Then, by Condition (ii),
there exists some
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all but countably many
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)\leq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B^{2\Delta})+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}\big(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n}\big)\mathbbm{I}\big(X_{n}\in B\text{ or }Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B\big)>\Delta\Big).

This allows us to pick a sequence
\delta_{k}\downarrow 0
such that
Condition (ii) of Lemma
A.3
holds under each
\delta=\delta_{k}
, which implies

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B^{2\Delta})+\limsup_{k\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}\big(X_{n},Y^{\delta_{k}}_{n}\big)\mathbbm{I}\big(X_{n}\in B\text{ or }Y^{\delta_{k}}_{n}\in B\big)>\Delta\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B^{2\Delta})\qquad\text{ by Condition (i).}

(A.1)

Analogously, observe the lower bound (for each
n\geq 1
and
\delta>0
)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}\big(X_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})\leq\Delta\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B_{\Delta};\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})\leq\Delta\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B_{\Delta})-\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B_{\Delta};\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})>\Delta\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B_{\Delta})-\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B\text{ or }X_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})>\Delta\big)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B_{\Delta},\ V^{\delta}_{n}=v)-\mathbf{P}\big(Y^{\delta}_{n}\in B\text{ or }X_{n}\in B;\ \bm{d}(X_{n},Y^{\delta}_{n})>\Delta\big).

Again, by Condition (ii),
one can pick a sequence
\delta_{k}\downarrow 0
such that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\delta_{k}}_{n}\in B_{\Delta},\ V^{\delta_{k}}_{n}=v)

\displaystyle\qquad-\limsup_{k\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}\big(X_{n},Y^{\delta_{k}}_{n}\big)\mathbbm{I}\big(X_{n}\in B\text{ or }Y^{\delta_{k}}_{n}\in B\big)>\Delta\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B_{2\Delta})\qquad\text{ by Conditions (i) and (ii)}.

(A.2)

Since

|\mathcal{V}|<\infty
,

\mu_{v}\in\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{C})
for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
,
and

B^{2\Delta}
is bounded away from
\mathbb{C}
,
we have
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B^{2\Delta})<\infty
.
By sending
\Delta\downarrow 0
in (
A.1
) and (
A.2
),
it then follows from the continuity of measures that

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B^{\circ})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in B)\leq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mu_{v}(B^{-}).

By the arbitrariness of our choice of
B
,
we conclude the proof using Theorem
A.2
.
∎

Appendix B
Details for Tail Asymptotics of Hawkes Process Clusters

To precisely define the limiting measures
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}
in (
2.14
),
we review a few definitions in
[
12
]
.

Definition B.1

(Type)
.

We say that
{\bm{I}}=(I_{k,j})_{k\geq 1,\ j\in[d]}
is a
type
if

•

I_{k,j}\in\{0,1\}
for each
k\geq 1
and
j\in[d]
;

•

there exists
\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
\sum_{j\in[d]}I_{k,j}=0\ \forall k>\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}
and
\sum_{j\in[d]}I_{k,j}\geq 1\ \forall 1\leq k\leq\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}
;

•

for each
j\in[d]
, we have
\sum_{k\geq 1}I_{k,j}\leq 1
;

•

for
k=1
, the set
\{j\in[d]:\ I_{1,j}=1\}
is either empty or contains exactly one element.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be the collection of all types.
For each
\bm{I}\in\mathscr{I}
, let

\displaystyle\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\big\{j\in[d]:\ I_{k,j}=1\text{ for some }k\geq 1\big\},\qquad\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\big\{j\in[d]:\ I_{k,j}=1\big\}\ \ \forall k\geq 1.

(B.1)

Intuitively speaking,

\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}
is the
depth
of
\bm{I}
,

\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}
is the set of
active indices
in
\bm{I}
, and
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}
is the set of active indices of
\bm{I}
at depth
k
.
For any
\bm{I}\in\mathscr{I}
, note that:
(i)
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}=\emptyset
(and hence
\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}=0
) if and only if
I_{k,j}\equiv 0
for any
k,j
;
and
(ii) when
\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}\geq 1
, there uniquely exists some
j^{\bm{I}}_{1}\in[d]
such that
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{1}=\{j^{\bm{I}}_{1}\}
.
Next,
given
\beta>0
, we define a Borel measure on
(0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle\nu_{\beta}(dw)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\frac{\beta dw}{w^{\beta+1}}\mathbbm{I}\{w>0\}.

(B.2)

Given non-empty sets
\mathcal{I}\subseteq[d]
and
\mathcal{J}\subseteq[d]
,
we say that

\{\mathcal{J}(i):\ i\in\mathcal{I}\}

is an
assignment of
\mathcal{J}
to
\mathcal{I}
if

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(i)\subseteq\mathcal{J}\quad\forall i\in\mathcal{I};\qquad\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{J}(i)=\mathcal{J};\qquad\mathcal{J}(i)\cap\mathcal{J}(i^{\prime})=\emptyset\quad\forall i\neq i^{\prime}.

(B.3)

We use
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\mathcal{J}}
to denote the set containing all assignments of
\mathcal{J}
to
\mathcal{I}
.
Given non-empty
\mathcal{I}\subseteq[d]
and
\mathcal{J}\subseteq[d]
,
define the mapping

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\mathcal{J}}(\bm{w})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{\{\mathcal{J}(i):\ i\in\mathcal{I}\}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\mathcal{J}}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}(i)}w_{i}\bar{s}_{l^{*}(j)\leftarrow i},\quad\forall\bm{w}=(w_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\in[0,\infty)^{|\mathcal{I}|}.

(B.4)

Here, recall that
\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}=\mathbf{E}\bm{S}_{j}
,
\bar{s}_{j\leftarrow i}=\mathbf{E}S_{j\leftarrow i}
(see (
2.10
)),
and
l^{*}(j)
is defined in (
2.11
).

Given a type
\bm{I}\in\mathscr{I}
whose active index set
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}
is non-empty,
recall that when
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}\neq\emptyset
,
there uniquely exists some
j^{\bm{I}}_{1}\in[d]
such that
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{1}=\{j^{\bm{I}}_{1}\}
.
Let

\displaystyle\nu^{\bm{I}}(d\bm{w})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bigtimes_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\bigg(\bigtimes_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}\nu_{\alpha^{*}(j)}(dw_{k,j})\bigg),

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\bm{I}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\int\mathbbm{I}\Bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\sum_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}w_{k,j}\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}\in\ \cdot\ \Bigg\}\Bigg(\bar{s}_{l^{*}(j^{\bm{I}}_{1})\leftarrow i}\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}-1}g_{\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}\leftarrow\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k+1}}(\bm{w}_{k})\Bigg)\nu^{\bm{I}}(d\bm{w}),

(B.6)

where we adopt the notations
\bm{w}_{k}=(w_{k,j})_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}

and
\bm{w}=(\bm{w}_{k})_{k\in[\mathcal{K}^{I}]}
.
Besides, note that
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{I}}_{i}(\cdot)
is supported on the cone
\mathbb{R}^{d}(\bm{j}^{\bm{I}})
(see (
2.2
)).
The measures
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}(\cdot)
in (
2.14
) admit the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{\bm{j}}_{i}=\sum_{\bm{I}\in\mathscr{I}:\ \bm{j}^{\bm{I}}=\bm{j}}\mathbf{C}^{\bm{I}}_{i}.

(B.7)

Next, continuing the discussions in Remark
6
,
we note that the evaluation of the limiting measures

\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}(\cdot)

in (
3.29
) can be addressed by rejection sampling for
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{I}}_{i}(\cdot)
in (
B.6
).
Indeed,
as noted in Remark
6
, the key task is to sample under the law
\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{j},\bar{\delta}}(\cdot)
in (
3.30
).
Furthermore, the evaluation of
\mathbf{C}^{\bm{I}}_{i}\big(\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\big)
is detailed in Remark 4 of
[
12
]
.
Then,
by (
3.25
) and (
B.7
),
it suffices to check how to sample from probability measures

\hat{\mathbf{C}}^{\bm{I}}_{i,\bar{\delta}}(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\mathbf{C}^{\bm{I}}_{i}\Big(\ \cdot\ \cap\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\Big)\Big/\mathbf{C}^{\bm{I}}_{i}\Big(\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})\Big),

where

\bm{I}\in\mathscr{I}
is such that
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}=\bm{j}
(see Definition
B.1
).
Furthermore,
the proof of Lemma 4.10 (b) of
[
12
]

implies the existence of some constant
M^{\prime}<\infty
such that (we write
\bm{w}_{k}=(w_{k,j})_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}
)

\Bigg[\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}-1}g_{\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}\leftarrow\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k+1}}(\bm{w}_{k})\Bigg]\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\prod_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}{w_{k,j}^{-\alpha^{*}(j)-1}}\leq M^{\prime}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\prod_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}{w_{k,j}^{-\alpha^{*}(j)}}

whenever

\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\sum_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}w_{k,j}\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta}).

Here,
the mapping
g_{\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\mathcal{J}}(\cdot)
is defined in (
B.4
),

\alpha^{*}(\cdot)
is defined in (
2.11
),
and
the
\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}
and
\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}
’s are specified in Definition
B.1
.
Then, by definitions in (
B.6
),
to sample from
\hat{\mathbf{C}}^{\bm{I}}_{i,\bar{\delta}}(\cdot)

it suffices to
set

\displaystyle\hat{g}^{\bm{I}}(\bm{w})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Bigg[\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}-1}g_{\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}\leftarrow\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k+1}}(\bm{w}_{k})\Bigg]\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\prod_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}{\bigg(\frac{\delta}{w_{k,j}}\bigg)^{\alpha^{*}(j)+1}},\quad\hat{f}^{\bm{I}}(\bm{w})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\prod_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}}\prod_{j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}{\bigg(\frac{\delta}{w_{k,j}}\bigg)^{\alpha^{*}(j)}},

pick
\delta>0
small enough and
M>0
large enough, and run rejection sampling as follows.

•

Independently
for each
k\in[\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}]
and
j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}
,
generate a Pareto (i.e., exact power-law) random variable
W^{(\delta)}_{k,j}
with lower bound
\delta
and power-law index
\alpha^{*}(j)-1
;
Write
\bm{W}^{(\delta)}=\big(W^{(\delta)}_{k,j}\big)_{k\in[\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}],j\in\bm{j}^{\bm{I}}_{k}}
.

•

Generate
U\sim\text{Unif}(0,1)
.

•

If
\sum_{k\in[\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}]}\sum_{j\in\bm{j}}W^{(\delta)}_{k,j}\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{>}(\bm{j},\bar{\delta})
and

U<\hat{g}^{\bm{I}}(\bm{W}^{(\delta)})\big/\big[M\cdot\hat{f}^{\bm{I}}(\bm{W}^{(\delta)})\big],

return

\sum_{k\in[\mathcal{K}^{\bm{I}}]}\sum_{j\in\bm{j}}W^{(\delta)}_{k,j}\bar{\bm{s}}_{j}.

Otherwise, rerun this procedure.

Appendix C
Counterexamples of Topology and Tail Behavior

Example 1
.

In this two-dimensional example, we demonstrate that the LDP (
3.29
) stated in Theorem
3.3
would generally fail w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
, the càdlàg space with compact domain.
The intuition behind the counterexample is that, for any immigrant arriving near the end of the time interval
[0,T]
, its cluster size vector may be arbitrarily truncated at time
T
due to randomness in the birth times of its descendants.
As a result, the LDP in (
3.29
) generally fails under the product
M_{1}
topology on
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
for rare event sets
B
that involve the value of the process at time
T
.

Without loss of generality, we fix
T=1
, and lighten the notations by writing

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}\big([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d}\big),\qquad{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}}=\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,1],\qquad{\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k}}}=\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}}[0,1],

\displaystyle{\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}}=\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,1]}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}},\qquad{\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}}=\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,1]}},\qquad{\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}}=\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}^{{}_{[0,1]}}.

We focus on the case with
d=2
and adopt all assumptions imposed in Theorem
3.3
.
Besides, we impose the following conditions on the fertility functions (see (
1.1
)):

\displaystyle f^{\bm{N}}_{1\leftarrow 1}(t)=0,

\displaystyle\qquad\forall t>1,

(C.1)

\displaystyle f^{\bm{N}}_{2\leftarrow 1}(t)=0,

\displaystyle\qquad\forall t\in[0,2].

(C.2)

Here, condition (
C.1
) implies that the time a type-1 parent waits to give birth to a type-1 child would, with probability 1, take values over
[0,1]
;
condition (
C.2
) implies that the time a type-1 parent waits to give birth to a type-2 child is always strictly larger than
2
.
We note that:
(i) it is easy to identify
concrete cases that are compatible with both (
C.1
)–(
C.2
)
and the
tail condition (
3.28
) in Theorem
3.3
under any
\bm{k}
;
for instance,
one can take
f^{\bm{N}}_{1\leftarrow 1}(t)=\mathbbm{I}_{(0,1)}(t)
, which induces a uniform distribution over
(0,1)
,
and
f^{\bm{N}}_{2\leftarrow 1}(t)=\mathbbm{I}_{(2,\infty)}(t)\cdot\exp(-(t-2))
,
which induces an exponential distribution with
+2
offset;
(ii)
the exact form of conditions (
C.1
)–(
C.2
) are not pivotal to the analysis below and are imposed only for simplicity;
for instance, the arguments below would still hold after minor modifications if we set
f^{\bm{N}}_{j\leftarrow i}(t)=A_{j\leftarrow i}\exp(-b_{j\leftarrow i}\cdot t)

(i.e., with exact exponential tails in decay functions, which make the Hawkes process Markovian).

Regarding the tail indices for the clusters, we assume that

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(1)=\alpha_{1\leftarrow 1}>1,\qquad\alpha_{2\leftarrow 1}>\alpha_{1\leftarrow 1},\qquad\alpha^{*}(2)>\alpha^{*}(1)+1,

(C.3)

where

\alpha_{i\leftarrow j}
is the regular variation index for
B_{i\leftarrow j}
(see Assumption
2
and the discussion right above) and

\alpha^{*}(\cdot)
is defined in (
2.11
).

We are interested in studying the asymptotics (as
n\to\infty
)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(n^{-1}\bm{N}(n)\in A\big)=\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big),

where
\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}=\{\bm{N}(nt)/n:\ t\in[0,1]\}
is the scaled sample path of the Hawkes process
\bm{N}(t)
embedded in
\mathbb{D}
, and

\displaystyle A\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\big\{(x_{1},x_{2})^{\text{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\ x_{1}>1,\ cx_{1}>x_{2}\big\},\qquad E\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\big\{\xi\in\mathbb{D}:\ \xi(1)\in A\big\}.

(C.4)

To specify the choice of
c
,
we note that
by Assumption
3
,
in
\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}=(\bar{s}_{1\leftarrow i},\bar{s}_{2\leftarrow i})^{\top}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
(see (
2.10
))
and
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}=(\mu_{\bm{N},1},\mu_{\bm{N},2})^{\top}
(see (
3.27
)),
all coordinates are strictly positive.
Therefore,
we can fix
c>0
small enough such that
the set

\{\bm{x}+\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}:\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{2}(\{1,2\},\epsilon)\}

is bounded away from
A
for some
\epsilon>0
;
see (
2.3
) for the definition of cones
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\epsilon)
.
More generally, given
a\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
M=M(c,a)\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\leq\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}\leq\begin{pmatrix}a\\
a\end{pmatrix},\ y>M\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}y\\
0\end{pmatrix}\in A.

(C.5)

We start by making a few observations.
Recall that
{\mathcal{P}}_{m}
is the collection of non-empty subsets of
[m]
,
and hence
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{1,2\}\}
.
Also, in the context of Theorem
3.3
, recall the definitions of

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}:\\
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\notin\mathbb{A}(\bm{k}),\ \breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})\leq c(\bm{k})\end{subarray}}\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}},\qquad\breve{c}(\bm{\mathcal{K}})=\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}\cdot\big(\bm{\alpha}(\bm{j})-1\big),\qquad c(\bm{k})=\sum_{i=1,2}k_{i}\cdot\big(\alpha(\{i\})-1\big),

(C.6)

where
\bm{\alpha}(\bm{j})=1+\sum_{i\in\bm{j}}\big(\alpha^{*}(i)-1\big)
is defined in (
2.12
).
In particular, note that
\alpha(\{1\})=\alpha^{*}(1)
,
\alpha(\{2\})=\alpha^{*}(2)
, and
\alpha(\{1,2\})=\alpha^{*}(1)+\alpha^{*}(2)-1
.
Also,
we introduce notations
\bm{\mathcal{K}}^{\ \bm{i}}=(\mathcal{K}^{\ \bm{i}}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}

by setting
\mathcal{K}^{\ \bm{i}}_{\bm{j}}=1
if
\bm{j}=\bm{i}
, and
\mathcal{K}^{\ \bm{i}}_{\bm{j}}=0
otherwise.
For instance, in
\bm{\mathcal{K}}^{\{1,2\}}=(\mathcal{K}^{\{1,2\}}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}
, we have
\mathcal{K}^{\{1,2\}}_{\{1,2\}}=1
and
\mathcal{K}^{\{1,2\}}_{\{1\}}=\mathcal{K}^{\{1,2\}}_{\{2\}}=0
.
By (
C.3
) and (
C.6
),
we have

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant(0,1)}\subseteq\bigcup_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}:\ \mathcal{K}_{\{2\}}=\mathcal{K}_{\{1,2\}}=0}\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}.

Furthermore, for any
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}}
such that
\mathcal{K}_{\{2\}}=\mathcal{K}_{\{1,2\}}=0

and any
\xi\in\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\xi(1)\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{2}(\{1\},\epsilon)+\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}.

Meanwhile,
recall the definitions of
the sets
A
and
E
in (
C.4
).
It has been noted above that

\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{2}(\{1,2\},\epsilon)+\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}

is bounded away from
A
,
which implies that the set
E
is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant(0,1)}
under
\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}}
.
Now, suppose that the asymptotics (
3.29
) stated in Theorem
3.3
hold w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}
(i.e., on the metric space
(\mathbb{D},\bm{d}_{\mathcal{P}})
).
Then,
we are led to believe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\breve{\lambda}_{(0,1)}(n)\Big),\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(C.7)

However, our analysis below disproves (
C.7
) by verifying that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big)\big/\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)>0.

(C.8)

Indeed, by Assumption
2
,
we have

\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-\alpha_{1\leftarrow 1}}(n);

by
(
C.3
) and (
C.6
),
we have

\breve{\lambda}_{(0,1)}(n)\in\mathcal{RV}_{-c((0,1))}(n)

with
c\big((0,1)\big)=\alpha^{*}(2)-1>\alpha_{1\leftarrow 1}
.
Then, by

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big)}{\breve{\lambda}_{(0,1)}(n)}\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big)}{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)}\cdot\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)}{\breve{\lambda}_{(0,1)}(n)},

we end up with

\liminf_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big)}\big/{\breve{\lambda}_{(0,1)}(n)}=\infty,

which is a clear contradiction to (
C.7
).
We thus confirm that it is generally not possible to strengthen
the characterizations in (
3.29
) stated in Theorem
3.3
w.r.t. the product
M_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)
to those on
\mathbb{D}[0,T]
.

Below, we verify Claim (
C.8
).
To proceed, we adopt the notations in (
E.24
)–(
E.28
)
for the cluster representation of Hawkes processes.
Specifically, for each
j\in\{1,2\}
,
we use the sequence
\big(T_{j;k}^{\mathcal{C}}\big)_{k\geq 1}
to denote the arrival times of type-
j
immigrants, which are generated by a Poisson process
G^{\bm{N}}_{j}(t)
with rate
c^{\bm{N}}_{j}

(see (
1.1
)).
For
\bm{N}^{\mathcal{O}}_{(T^{\mathcal{C}}_{j;k},j)}
, the cluster process induced by the
k^{\text{th}}
type-
j
immigrant,
we denote its size by

\displaystyle S^{(k)}_{i\leftarrow j}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{m\geq 0}\mathbbm{I}\Big\{A^{\mathcal{O}}_{j;k}(m)=i\Big\},\qquad\bm{S}^{(k)}_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\Big(S^{(k)}_{1\leftarrow j},S^{(k)}_{2\leftarrow j},\ldots,S^{(k)}_{d\leftarrow j}\Big)^{\text{T}}.

Next, we define the event (with
\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}
defined in (
3.27
))

\displaystyle\text{(I)}=\Bigg\{n^{-1}\sum_{j\in\{1,2\}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1:T_{j;k}^{\mathcal{C}}\leq n-2\end{subarray}}\bm{S}^{(k)}_{j}\leq 2\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}\Bigg\},

which represents the case where the accumulated size of all clusters arrived by time
n-2
is upper bounded by the vector
2n\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}}
.
Also, recall that we use
G^{\bm{N}}_{j}(t)
to denote the Poisson process generating the arrival times
T^{\mathcal{C}}_{j;k}
for the immigrants.
Let

\displaystyle\text{(II)}=\Big\{G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(n-1)-G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(n-2)=1;\ G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(n)-G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(n-1)=0;\ G^{\bm{N}}_{2}(n)-G^{\bm{N}}_{2}(n-2)=0\Big\}.

That is, on event (II), there is only one type-
1
immigrant who arrived during the time window
(n-2,n]
, with the arrival time lying in
(n-2,n-1]
, and no type-
2
immigrant arrived during
(n-2,n]
.
On this event, for the only type-
1
immigrant that arrived during
(n-2,n]
, we use
B_{1\leftarrow 1}
to specifically denote the number of its type-
1
children (in one generation).
Let

\displaystyle\text{(III)}=\big\{B_{1\leftarrow 1}>nM\big\}.

Here, by (
C.5
), we fix some
M
large enough such that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\leq\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}\leq 2\bm{\mu}_{\bm{N}},\ y>M\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}y\\
0\end{pmatrix}\in A.

(C.9)

Now, we make some observations.
First, on the event
(II)
,
by (
C.1
)
we know that all the
B_{1\leftarrow 1}
children of the type-
1
ancestor must have arrived by time
n
,
and they may further give birth to more type-
1
offspring by time
n
.
On the other hand, by (
C.2
),
we know that any type-
2
offspring in this cluster, induced by the type-
1
immigrant who arrived during
(n-2,n]
, will have to arrive after time
n
.
Then, by (
C.9
), on event
\text{(I)}\cap\text{(II)}\cap\text{(III)}
we must have
n^{-1}\bm{N}(n)\in A
.
This leads to

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}_{n}\in E\big)}{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)}=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}\big(n^{-1}\bm{N}(n)\in A\big)}{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)}\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\text{(I)}\big)\cdot\mathbf{P}\big(\text{(II)}\big)\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>nM)}{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)},

where the inequality follows from the independent and stationary increments in Poisson processes
G^{\bm{N}}_{j}(t)
.
By the law of large numbers, we get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\text{(I)}\big)=1.

By the law of Poisson processes, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(\text{(II)}\big)=\mathbf{P}\Big(G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(1)=1;\ G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(2)-G^{\bm{N}}_{1}(1)=0;\ G^{\bm{N}}_{2}(2)=0\Big)>0.

Lastly, by the regularly varying law of
B_{1\leftarrow 1}
, we get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>nM)}{\mathbf{P}(B_{1\leftarrow 1}>n)}=M^{-\alpha_{1\leftarrow 1}}>0.

This concludes the proof of Claim (
C.8
).

Example 2
.

This example demonstrates that, even in the univariate setting, we need sufficiently tight power-law bounds on the tail behavior of the decay functions (like in (
3.28
)) for the large deviations asymptotics stated in Theorem
3.3
to hold.
The intuition of this is that, without sufficiently tight tail bounds on the decay functions, some rare events may be driven by a single large cluster with a long lifetime—effectively a big jump spread broadly across the entire timeline—rather than by multiple large clusters.

Specifically, we consider a univariate Hawkes process
N(t)
with initial value
N(0)=0
and conditional intensity

h^{N}(t)=c^{N}+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{B}(s)f^{N}(t-s)dN(s),

where the constant
c^{N}>0
is the immigration rate,
the decay function
f^{N}(\cdot)
takes the form

\displaystyle f^{N}(t)=\frac{\theta}{(1+t)^{\theta+1}},\qquad\forall t\geq 0,

(C.10)

for some
\theta>1

(i.e.,
\int_{t}^{\infty}f^{N}(s)ds=(1+t)^{-\theta}
, and
\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{N}(t)dt=1
),
and the excitation rates
\tilde{B}(s)
are independent copies of some non-negative random variable
\tilde{B}

with law

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\tilde{B}>x)=\frac{1}{\max\{1,x/\tilde{c}\}^{\alpha}},\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

(C.11)

Here, we have
\alpha>1
for the power-law tail index;
the constant
\tilde{c}>0
is small enough such that
\mathbf{E}[\tilde{B}]\cdot\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1}=\mathbf{E}[\tilde{B}]<1
(so the sub-critical condition in Assumption
1
also holds).
By (
3.27
), in this univariate setting we have

\mu_{N}=\frac{c^{N}}{1-\mathbf{E}[\tilde{B}]\cdot\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1}}=\frac{c^{N}}{1-\mathbf{E}[\tilde{B}]}.

Recall that

\bar{N}_{n}(t)=N(nt)/n
,
and that

\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\bar{N}_{n}(t):\ t\geq 0\}
is the scaled sample path of the Hawkes process
N(t)
.
In this example, we study events of the form

\{\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in A(k)\}

with

\displaystyle A(k)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\big\{\xi\in\mathbb{D}\big([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}\big):\ \xi(t_{l}+u)-\xi(t_{l})>\mu_{N}\ \forall l=0,1,\ldots,k-1\big\},

(C.12)

where

•

k
is some positive integer;

•

the
t_{l}
’s are such that

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{k-1}<1
;

•

Furthermore,
u\in(0,1)
is small enough such that, across
l=0,1,\ldots,k-1
,
the intervals
I_{l}=[t_{l},t_{l}+u]
are mutually disjoint and are all contained within
[0,1]
.

In other words, for any path
\xi
that belongs to the set
A(k)
, the increment of
\xi
over each of the mutually disjoint intervals
I_{l}
is bounded from below by
\mu_{N}
.
In particular, note that

A(k)
is bounded away from (by adapting (
3.7
) and (
3.17
) to the univariate setting)

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant k;\mu_{N}}[0,\infty)=\bigcup_{k^{\prime}=0,1,\ldots,k-1}\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{k^{\prime};\mu_{N}}[0,\infty)=\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{k-1;\mu_{N}}[0,\infty)

under
\bm{d}_{M_{1}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}

for any
\epsilon>0
small enough:
indeed,
a linear path of slope
\mu_{N}
would only increase by
u\cdot\mu_{N}<\mu_{N}
over each interval
I_{l}
,
meaning that at least one jump needs to be added to the linear path over each
(I_{l})_{l=0,1,\ldots,k-1}
for it to enter the set
A(k)
.
Also, when there is no ambiguity about the choice of
k
, we simply write
A=A(k)
.

We note that, regardless of the value of the tail indices
\theta
in (
C.10
) for the decay function and
\alpha
in (
C.11
) for the law of the excitation rates,
the tail condition (
3.28
) would be violated eventually for all
k
large enough.
Indeed, by (
2.13
) and (
3.15
),
in this univariate setting we have

\displaystyle\breve{\lambda}_{k}(x)=\Big(x\mathbf{P}(\tilde{B}>x)\Big)^{k}=\mathcal{O}(x^{-k(\alpha-1)}).

Therefore, condition (
3.28
) fails if
\theta<k(\alpha-1)+1
.
Suppose that Theorem
3.3
holds even after dropping the tail condition (
3.28
) on the decay functions,
then we are led to believe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in A\big)=\mathcal{O}\big(\breve{\lambda}_{k}(n)\big)=\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-k(\alpha-1)}\big),\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(C.13)

However, our analysis below derives the lower bound

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in A\big)\cdot n^{\alpha(\theta+1)-1}>0.

(C.14)

This clearly contradicts (
C.13
) for all
k
large enough such that
k(\alpha-1)>\alpha(\theta+1)-1
.
In summary, even in the univariate setting,
it is generally not possible to remove
tail bounds on the decay functions from Theorem
3.3
.

The rest of this example is devoted to establishing the lower bound (
C.14
),
and
we proceed by defining a few events.
Let
0<T_{1}<T_{2}<\ldots
be a sequence generated by a Poisson process with rate
c^{N}
.
This sequence denotes the arrival time sequence of the immigrants in
N(t)
.
Also, let
(B_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be independent copies of

B\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.01042pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$}}\hskip-3.01042pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\text{Poisson}(\tilde{B}\cdot\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.01042pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$}}\hskip-3.01042pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\text{Poisson}(\tilde{B})

(recall that we have
\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1}=1
).
Note that
\mathbf{P}(B>x)\sim\mathbf{P}(\tilde{B}>x)
as
x\to\infty
.
In the cluster representation of
N(t)
, each
B_{i}
denotes the count of first-generation offspring of the immigrant arriving at time
T_{i}
.
We fix
C\in(0,\infty)
large enough such that

\displaystyle\frac{C\theta u}{4}>\mu_{N},

(C.15)

and
define the event

\displaystyle(A:*)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigg\{\exists i\text{ such that }T_{i}\leq\frac{nu}{2},\ B_{i}>Cn^{\theta+1}\bigg\}.

(C.16)

That is, on the event
(A:*)
, an immigrant arrives before time
\frac{nu}{2}
and plans to give birth to more than
n^{\theta+1}
descendants in one generation.
Next, on this event, we use
i^{*}
to denote the smallest
i
such that the conditions in (
C.16
) hold.
Let the sequence
(T^{(i^{*})}_{k})_{k\geq 1}

be independent copies with density
f^{N}(\cdot)/\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1}=f^{N}(\cdot)
;
this sequence denotes the gap between the arrival times of the
i^{*}
-th immigrant and its first-generation offspring.
Using these notations, we define the event

\displaystyle(A:0)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigg\{\#\big\{k\leq\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil:\ T^{(i^{*})}_{k}\leq nu/2\big\}>\mu_{N}\cdot n\bigg\}.

(C.17)

On the event
(A:*)\cap(A:0)
, note that in the Hawkes process
N(t)

we must have more than
\mu_{\bm{N}}\cdot n
points arriving by time
nu
.
Indeed, on this event, there is an immigrant who arrives by time
nu/2
and gives birth to more than
n\cdot\mu_{N}
of its first-generation descendants within
nu/2
time after its arrival.
Then, under the
\mathcal{O}(n)
scaling for both time and space in
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
,
we have
\bar{N}_{n}(u)-\bar{N}_{n}(0)>\mu_{N}
.
Besides, for each
l\in[k-1]
we define

\displaystyle(A:l)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\Bigg\{\#\bigg\{k\leq\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil:\ T^{(i^{*})}_{k}\in\bigg[nt_{l},\ nt_{l}+\frac{nu}{2}\bigg]\bigg\}>\mu_{N}\cdot n\Bigg\}.

(C.18)

Similarly, on the event
(A:*)\cap(A:l)
we must have more than
\mu_{N}\cdot n
points arriving during
[nt_{l},n(t_{l}+u)]
.
Then, under the
\mathcal{O}(n)
scaling for both time and space in
\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}
,
we have
\bar{N}_{n}(t_{l}+u)-\bar{N}_{n}(t_{l})>\mu_{N}
.
In summary,
on the event
(A:*)\cap\big(\bigcup_{l=0,1,\ldots,k}(A:l)\big)
,
we have

\bar{\bm{N}}^{{}_{[0,\infty)}}_{n}\in A.

Furthermore, if we can verify that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big((A:*)\big)\cdot n^{\alpha(\theta+1)-1}

\displaystyle>0,

(C.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big((A:l)\ \big|\ (A:*)\big)

\displaystyle=1,\qquad\forall l=0,1,\ldots,k-1,

(C.20)

then, the lower bound (
C.14
) follows immediately.

Verification of Claim (
C.19
)
.
By the law of a compound Poisson process,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big((A:*)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Poisson}\Big(\frac{un}{2}\cdot c^{N}\cdot\mathbf{P}(B>Cn^{\theta+1})\Big)\geq 1\bigg)=1-\exp\bigg(-\frac{c^{N}u}{2}\cdot n\mathbf{P}(B>Cn^{\theta+1})\bigg).

Then, by
\mathbf{P}(B>x)\sim\mathbf{P}(\tilde{B}>x)
as
x\to\infty
and (
C.11
),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big((A:*)\big)\cdot n^{\alpha(\theta+1)-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{N}u}{2}\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{\alpha(\theta+1)-1}\cdot{n\mathbf{P}(B>Cn^{\theta+1})}=\frac{c^{N}u}{2}\cdot\big(C/\tilde{c}\big)^{-\alpha}>0.

Verification of Claim (
C.20
), (
l=0
)
.
We first verify the case where
l=0
.
Since the
T^{(i^{*})}_{k}
’s are independent copies with density
f^{N}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:0)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Binomial}\Big(\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil,\int_{0}^{nu/2}f^{N}(t)dt\Big)\leq\mu_{N}n\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Binomial}\Big(\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil,1/2\Big)\leq\mu_{N}n\bigg)\quad\text{for any $n$ large enough due to }\left\lVert f^{N}\right\rVert_{1}=1

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Binomial}\Big(\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil,1/2\Big)\leq\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil/4\bigg)\quad\text{for any $n$ large enough due to $\theta>0$}.

Then, by the law of large numbers,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:0)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Binomial}\Big(\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil,1/2\Big)<\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil/4\bigg)=0.

This verifies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big((A:0)\ \big|\ (A:*)\big)=1.

Verification of Claim (
C.20
), (
l=1,2,\ldots,k-1
)
.
Next, we take any
l\in[k-1]
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:l)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Binomial}\Big(\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil,\underbrace{\int_{nt_{l}}^{n(t_{l}+\frac{u}{2})}f^{N}(t)dt}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.06946pt\hskip-3.06946pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.06946pt\hskip-2.23608pt\raisebox{4.3691pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-2.23608pt\hskip 3.06946pt}}F_{l}}\Big)\leq\mu_{N}n\bigg).

By (
C.10
)
and that
t_{l}+u<1
,

\displaystyle F_{l}=(nt_{l})^{-\theta}-\bigg(nt_{l}+\frac{nu}{2}\bigg)^{-\theta}\geq\frac{\theta}{(1+n)^{\theta+1}}\cdot\frac{nu}{2}.

As a result,
for any
n
large enough we have

F_{l}\geq\frac{\theta u}{4}\cdot n^{-\theta}
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:l)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)\leq\mathbf{P}\bigg(\text{Binomial}\Big(\lceil Cn^{\theta+1}\rceil,\frac{\theta u}{4}\cdot n^{-\theta}\Big)\leq\mu_{N}n\bigg).

By Chernoff bounds, for any
w>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:l)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)\leq\exp(w\mu_{N}n)\cdot\bigg(1-\frac{\theta u}{4n^{\theta}}+\frac{\theta ue^{-w}}{4n^{\theta}}\bigg)^{Cn^{\theta+1}}.

Then, due to
(1+x_{n})^{n}\sim\exp(nx_{n})
when
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=0
,
we get (for any
w>0
)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:l)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(-Cn^{\theta+1}\cdot(1-e^{-w})\cdot\frac{\theta u}{4n^{\theta}}+w\mu_{N}n\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-n\cdot\Big(\frac{C\theta u(1-e^{-w})}{4}-w\mu_{N}\Big)\bigg).

By our choice of
C
in (
C.15
),
it holds for any
w>0
small enough that

\frac{C\theta u(1-e^{-w})}{4}-w\mu_{N}>0,

which implies

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\big((A:l)\big)^{c}\ \Big|\ (A:*)\Big)=0

and hence

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big((A:l)\ \big|\ (A:*)\big)=1.

Appendix D
Proof for Large Deviations of Lévy Processes with
\mathcal{MHRV}
Increments

Without loss of generality, we prove Theorem
3.2
for
T=1
.
Besides, considering the arbitrariness of the drift constant
\bm{c}_{\bm{L}}
in (
3.4
),
we can w.l.o.g. impose the following assumption and focus on centered Lévy processes for the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Assumption 6

(WLOG Assumption for Theorem
3.2
)
.

\bm{\mu}_{\bm{L}}=\bm{0}
in (
3.4
), and
T=1
.

Henceforth in Section
D
, we also lighten notations in the proofs by writing

\displaystyle\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{0}}[0,1],\quad\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k};\bm{0}}[0,1],\quad\breve{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\breve{\mathbf{C}}^{{}_{[0,1]}}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{0}},\quad\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}^{{}_{[0,1]}},\quad\bm{d}_{J_{1}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\bm{d}_{J_{1}}^{{}_{[0,1]}}.

(D.1)

D.1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We start by identifying the
large jumps
in
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}
.
Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta>0
, let

\displaystyle\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\inf\bigg\{t>\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k-1):\ \left\lVert\Delta\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)\right\rVert>\delta\bigg\},\ \ \forall k\geq 1,\qquad\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(0)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}0;

(D.2)

\displaystyle\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Delta\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}\Big(\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k)\Big),\qquad\forall k\geq 1.

(D.3)

In (
D.2
), note that
\Delta\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)=\Delta\bm{L}(nt)/n
.
Intuitively speaking, the sequence
\big(\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k)\big)_{k\geq 1}
marks the arrival times of jumps in
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)
with size (in terms of
L_{1}
norm) larger than
\delta
, which correspond to jumps of size larger than
n\delta
in
\bm{L}_{n}(t)
;
the sequence
\big({\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k)}\big)_{k\geq 1}
represents the sizes of the large jumps.

Building upon the definitions in (
D.2
)–(
D.3
),
we introduce a
large-jump approximation
for
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)
.
Specifically, given
\delta>0
and
n\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}(t)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\sum_{k\geq 1}\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k)\mathbbm{I}_{[\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k),1]}(t),\qquad\forall t\in[0,1],

(D.4)

and

\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\{\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}(t):\ t\in[0,1]\}.

Clearly,
{\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}(t)}
is a step function (i.e., piece-wise constant) in
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}\big([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
that vanishes at the origin and approximates
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)
by only keeping the large jumps (under threshold
\delta
).

Our proof of Theorem
3.2

hinges on
Propositions
D.2
and
D.3

that outline the following key steps:
(i) first, we establish the asymptotic equivalence between
\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}
and
\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}
as in Lemma
A.3
;
(ii) next, we provide a detailed asymptotic analysis for the law of large jumps.
To carry out these two steps, we further classify the large jumps
\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k)
based on their locations
w.r.t. some cones
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})
.
Specifically, let

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}:\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta,\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\delta)\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta)\big\},\qquad\forall\delta>0,\ \bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}.

(D.5)

We highlight several useful properties regarding
{\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})}
.

•

First, note that
\bigcup_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}:\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta,\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}([d],\delta)\}
.
Indeed,
it is obvious that

\bigcup_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\subseteq\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}:\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta,\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}([d],\delta)\}
.
On the other hand, given any
\delta>0
and

\bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}([d],\delta)
with
\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta
,
the argument minimum

\displaystyle\bm{j}(\bm{x})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\underset{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}:\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\delta)}{\arg\min}\alpha(\bm{j})

uniquely exists under
the condition
\alpha(\bm{j})\neq\alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\ \forall\bm{j},\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}

in Assumption
5
.
Then,
we must have
\bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j}(\bm{x}))
due to the definition of
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta)=\bigcup_{\bm{j}^{\prime}\subseteq[k]:\ \bm{j}^{\prime}\neq\bm{j},\ \alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})}\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j}^{\prime},\delta).

This confirms that

\bigcup_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\supseteq\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}:\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta,\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}([d],\delta)\}
.

•

Next, we note that
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\cap\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j}^{\prime})=\emptyset
holds for any
\bm{j},\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
with
\bm{j}\neq\bm{j}^{\prime}
.
To see why, we assume
w.l.o.g. that
\alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})
.
Then, due to
\bm{j}^{\prime}\neq\bm{j}
and
\alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})
,
we have
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j}^{\prime})\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta)
.
Therefore, for any
\bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j}^{\prime})
, we must have

\bm{x}\notin\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\delta)\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta)
.
Repeating this argument for all
\bm{j},\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
,
we confirm that
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\cap\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j}^{\prime})=\emptyset
.

•

In summary,
the collection of sets
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})
provide a partition of
\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}:\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta,\ \bm{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}([d],\delta)\}
.
Meanwhile, note that

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\Big(\partial{\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})}\Big)=0,\qquad\text{for all but countably many }\delta>0,

(D.6)

where we use
\partial E\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}E^{-}\setminus E^{\circ}
to denote the boundary of the set
E
.
This claim is a consequence of the definition of
\mathcal{MHRV}
,
and we state the proof below.

Lemma D.1
.

Let Assumption
5
hold.
Let
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})
be defined as in (
D.5
).
For each
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\Big(\partial{\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})}\Big)=0,\qquad\text{for all but countably many }\delta>0.

(D.7)

Proof.

Take any
\delta_{0}>0
.
For each
\delta>\delta_{0}
, note that

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})

\displaystyle\subseteq\underbrace{\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}):\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert=\delta\big\}}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.06946pt\hskip-3.06946pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.06946pt\hskip-2.28471pt\raisebox{3.68855pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-2.28471pt\hskip 3.06946pt}}E_{1}(\delta)}\cup\underbrace{\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}):\ \bm{x}\in\partial\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j},\delta),\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta_{0}\big\}}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.06946pt\hskip-3.06946pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.06946pt\hskip-2.28471pt\raisebox{3.68855pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-2.28471pt\hskip 3.06946pt}}E_{2}(\delta)}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\underbrace{\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}):\ \bm{x}\in\partial\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta),\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta_{0}\big\}}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.06946pt\hskip-3.06946pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.06946pt\hskip-2.28471pt\raisebox{3.68855pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-2.28471pt\hskip 3.06946pt}}E_{3}(\delta)}.

We first analyze the set
E_{1}(\delta)
.
Note that
E_{1}(\delta)\cap E_{1}(\delta^{\prime})=\emptyset
for any
\delta_{0}<\delta<\delta^{\prime}
, and

\bigcup_{\delta>\delta_{0}}E_{1}(\delta)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}).

Meanwhile,
under the
\mathcal{MHRV}
condition in Assumption
5
,
we have

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\Big(\big(\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0})\big)^{c}\Big)<\infty,\qquad,\delta>0;

see Definition
2.1
.
As a result, given any
\epsilon>0
, there exists at most finitely many
\delta\in(\delta_{0},\infty)
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\big(E_{1}(\delta)\big)>\epsilon
.
Sending
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, we confirm that

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\big(E_{1}(\delta)\big)=0

for all but countably many
\delta\in(\delta_{0},\infty)
.

Similarly, note that

\bigcup_{\delta>\delta_{0}}E_{2}(\delta)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}),

and that

E_{2}(\delta)\cap E_{2}(\delta^{\prime})=\emptyset

for any
\delta^{\prime}>\delta>\delta_{0}
.
The same arguments above confirm that

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\big(E_{2}(\delta)\big)=0

for all but countably many
\delta\in(\delta_{0},\infty)
.
Next,
by the definition of
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta)
,
we have
E_{3}(\delta)\subseteq\bigcup_{\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}:\ \bm{j}^{\prime}\neq\bm{j},\ \alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})}E^{\bm{j}^{\prime}}_{3}(\delta)
, where

\displaystyle E^{\bm{j}^{\prime}}_{3}(\delta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}):\ \bm{x}\in\partial\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(\bm{j}^{\prime},\delta),\ \left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert>\delta_{0}\big\}.

For each
\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
with
\bm{j}^{\prime}\neq\bm{j},\ \alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})
,
one can see that

\bigcup_{\delta\in(\delta_{0},\infty)}E_{3}^{\bm{j}^{\prime}}(\delta)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\leqslant}(\bm{j},\delta_{0}),

and

E_{3}^{\bm{j}^{\prime}}(\delta)\cap E_{3}^{\bm{j}^{\prime}}(\delta^{\prime})=\emptyset

for any
\delta^{\prime}>\delta>\delta_{0}
.
Again, we get

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\big(E_{3}(\delta)\big)\leq\sum_{\bm{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}:\ \bm{j}^{\prime}\neq\bm{j},\ \alpha(\bm{j}^{\prime})\leq\alpha(\bm{j})}\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\big(E_{3}^{\bm{j}^{\prime}}(\delta)\big)=0

for all but countably many
\delta\in(\delta_{0},\infty)
.

In summary, we have verified that the claim

\mathbf{C}_{\bm{j}}\big(\partial\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\big)=0

holds
for all but countably many
\delta\in(\delta_{0},\infty)
.
We conclude the proof by sending
\delta_{0}\downarrow 0
.
∎

To proceed, given
\delta>0
,
\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let (for each
k\geq 1
)

\displaystyle\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\inf\Big\{t>\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k-1;\bm{j}):\ \Delta\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})\Big\},

(D.8)

\displaystyle\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Delta\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}\Big(\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})\Big),

(D.9)

and we adopt the convention
\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(0;\bm{j})=0
.
Analogous to the definitions in (
D.2
)–(
D.3
), the
{\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})}
’s and
{\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})}
’s are the arrival times and sizes of the
k^{\text{th}}
large jump that belongs to
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{>\delta}(\bm{j})
.
To provide the individual count for different types of large jumps, we define

\displaystyle K^{>\delta}_{n}(\bm{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\max\{k\geq 0:\ \tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})\leq 1\},\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d},

(D.10)

and keep track of all counts by defining

\displaystyle\bm{K}^{>\delta}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\Big({K^{>\delta}_{n}(\bm{j})}\Big)_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}.

(D.11)

Similarly, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\tau}^{>\delta}_{n}(k)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\inf\big\{t>\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k-1):\ \Delta\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}(t)\notin\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}([d],\delta)\big\},\qquad\tilde{\tau}^{>\delta}_{n}(0)=0,

(D.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{>\delta}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\max\{k\geq 0:\ \widetilde{\tau}^{>\delta}_{n}(k)\leq 1\},

(D.13)

for big jumps that are not aligned with any direction of the cone
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{d}([d],\delta)
.
Given
\bm{\mathcal{K}}=(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}})_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}
,
note that on the event
\{{\bm{K}^{>\delta}_{n}}=\bm{\mathcal{K}}\}\cap\{\widetilde{K}^{>\delta}_{n}=0\}
,
the process
\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}(t)
admits the form

\displaystyle{\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}(t)}=\sum_{\bm{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{d}}\sum_{k\in[\mathcal{K}_{\bm{j}}]}\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})\mathbbm{I}_{[\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j}),1]}(t),\qquad\forall t\in[0,1]

(D.14)

with the
{\tau^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})}
’s and
{\bm{W}^{>\delta}_{n}(k;\bm{j})}
’s defined in
(
D.8
)–(
D.9
).
In particular, on the event
\{{\bm{K}^{>\delta}_{n}}=\bm{\mathcal{K}}\}\cap\{\widetilde{K}^{>\delta}_{n}=0\}
,
we have
\hat{\bm{L}}^{>\delta}_{n}\in\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\delta}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}=\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\delta}_{\bm{\mathcal{K}};\bm{0}}[0,1],
;
see (
3.7
).

We are now ready to state Propositions
D.2
and
D.3
.
Specifically, let

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{K}}^{>\delta}_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big({\bm{K}}^{>\delta}_{n},\widetilde{K}^{\delta}_{n}\big)

(D.15)

be the concatenation of
\bm{K}^{>\delta}_{n}
and
\widetilde{K}^{>\delta}_{n}
defined in
(
D.10
)–(
D.13
).
Let

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{A}}(\bm{k})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.49658pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-4.49658pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-4.49658pt\hskip 4.49658pt}}\big\{(\bm{\mathcal{K}},0):\ \bm{\mathcal{K}}\in{\mathbb{A}}(\bm{k})\big\},\qquad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+},

(D.16)

where
\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
is introduced in Definition
3.1
.
That is, we augment each allocation
\bm{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathbb{A}(\bm{k})
with the same constant
0
.

Proposition D.2
.

Let Assumptions
5
and
6
hold.
For each
\bm{k}=(k_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
and
\Delta>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\bm{d}_{J_{1}}\big(\hat{\bm{L}}_{n}^{>\delta},\bar{\bm{L}}_{n}\big)>\Delta\Big)\Big/\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)=0,\qquad\forall\delta>0\text{ small enough,}

(D.17)

where
\breve{\lambda}_{\bm{k}}(n)
is defined in (
3.12
).

Proposition D.3
.

Let Assumptions
5
and
6
hold.
Let
f:\mathbb{D}\to[0,\infty)
be bounded (i.e.,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.19441pt\raisebox{5.64096pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{def}$}}\hskip-3.19441pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{D}}|f(\xi)|<\infty
) and continuous (w.r.t. the
J_{1}
topology of
\mathbb{D}
).
Let
\bm{k}=(k_{j})_{j\in[d]}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
and
\epsilon>0
.
Suppose
that

B=\text{supp}(f)

is bounded away from
\breve{\mathbb{D}}^{\epsilon}_{\leqslant\bm{k}}

under
\bm{d}_{J_{1}}
.
Then,

1.

for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
,
